Discussion Name:
Should humans act to fight climate change?

1. Thesis:
Humans should act to fight climate change.

  1.1. Con:
  The causes, effects, and nature of climate change are still heavily disputed.

    1.1.1. Pro:
    The climate is known to exhibit chaotic [1] behavior, thus it is difficult to link cause and effect.

      1.1.1.1. Pro:
      Earth's weather systems are too chaotic and complex [2] to allow confidence in long-term predictions based only on statistics [3].

        1.1.1.1.1. Con:
        Though the weather is chaotic, the climate is less so [4], as it is an average of weather patterns over long periods of time. This means that it is easier to spot patterns in the chaotic system.

        1.1.1.1.2. Pro:
        All IPCC scenarios rely on GDP growth hypotheses which are not guaranteed (p. 2 [5]).

        1.1.1.1.3. Pro:
        Climate science has been proven to be an inexact science and projected figures have been shown to be somewhat arbitrary [6].

        1.1.1.1.4. Con:
        Predictions based on statistics are increasingly accurate with larger sample sizes [7]. Similarly, when considering the weather, while it can be difficult to predict the outcome on a single day, predicting longer trends (like summer is warmer) is relatively easy.

      1.1.1.2. Pro:
      Some scientists believe that, as the global climate is a dynamic system, it can correct itself.

        1.1.1.2.1. Con:
        While wide variation in past climate conditions implies that the global climate is not 'self-correcting' or tightly bound to a specific temperature range, the severity of the outcome if it were could be extreme. As such, humanity should act to preempt disaster.

        1.1.1.2.2. Con:
        Theories of a self-correcting climate system have long been disproven [8].

        1.1.1.2.3. Pro:
        Increases in warming create more cloud cover, which can reduce warming. This shows that the Earth's temperatures could be regulated to some extent (p. 2817 [9]).

          1.1.1.2.3.1. Con:
          Water vapor is a greenhouse gas [10] with a strong positive [11] feedback effect that enforces global warming, rather than regulating it. As such, while some heat energy may be blocked by cloud cover, the Earth is still insulated.

          1.1.1.2.3.2. Pro:
          Scientists can continue to reduce warming by artificially brightening clouds [12] - this may help the climate to continue to correct high CO₂ emissions.

      1.1.1.3. Con:
      Meaningful, true claims can still be made about complex and chaotic systems; some can even have obvious simple connections [13] between cause and effect.

        1.1.1.3.1. Pro:
        There is a well-established field of study dedicated to complex systems [14] demonstrating that it is possible to make reliable claims in these areas.

          1.1.1.3.1.1. Pro:
          The physical constraints imposed by conservation of energy (i.e., more energy flowing into the system than out to space) is why long-term statistical prediction of warming is reliable while similar prediction of exact local states (weather) is not. In the terminology of chaos systems, the system is governed in the long run by its "boundary conditions [15]".

        1.1.1.3.2. Con:
        While meaningful and true claims could be made of complex chaotic systems, the difficulty in doing so makes it less likely that the outcomes of scientific modelling are unreliable [16] and cannot solely be used to justify drastic action.

        1.1.1.3.3. Pro:
        Even in chaotic systems, patterns can be observed, as is the case for AGW [17]. It is clear that the climate is becoming less suitable for humans, due to the increased rate of desertification [18], global temperature increases [19], and more frequent extreme [20] weather.

    1.1.2. Pro:
    Some scientists [21] disagree that climate change is human-made; as such, different approaches are suggested.

      1.1.2.1. Con:
      Even if the models are wrong and climate change is natural, uncertainty about changes in the climate prompt action to avoid large scale detrimental effects (p. 1 [22]).

        1.1.2.1.1. Con:
        The environment's capacity to absorb and sequester carbon [23] has so far kept pace with increasing greenhouse gas emissions.

          1.1.2.1.1.1. Con:
          Climate change may make carbon sinks less effective [24].

          1.1.2.1.1.2. Pro:
          Moss and peatlands have been highly effective at absorbing CO₂ and may have reduced anthropogenic global warming to some extent (p. 1 [25]).

          1.1.2.1.1.3. Con:
          Estimates have calculated that only ~26% of CO₂ released from human activity over 2002–2011 was absorbed by the oceans (p. 1150 [26]), causing a number of issues [27].

        1.1.2.1.2. Pro:
        Even if climate change wasn't caused by humans, this does not negate the fact that humans will be impacted by its consequences.

        1.1.2.1.3. Pro:
        With temperatures continuing to rise [28] even as emissions fall, global climate corrections may take a long time.

          1.1.2.1.3.1. Pro:
          The Gulf Stream [29] is likely to weaken in the 21st century and may cause disruptions in weather patterns to devastating effect [30].

      1.1.2.2. Pro:
      Climate change has not been adequately substantiated by science. Some doubt [31] the scientific consensus.

        1.1.2.2.1. Con:
        Few of the scientists who dispute [32] anthropogenic causes of climate change do so based on sound research. Their views should therefore be discounted.

        1.1.2.2.2. Pro:
        There is competing evidence about the scale and severity of warming caused by human CO₂ emissions, which may undercut projections made by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC [33]).

          1.1.2.2.2.1. Pro:
          CO₂ levels lag behind temperature, yet are considered to be the driving factor. This undermines the strength of AGW theory [34] on global temperature rises.

            1.1.2.2.2.1.1. Con:
            Temperature variations across the past century are consistent with those within the 1000 year climate development [35]. They are not chaotic enough to explain the eventual overall increase in global temperature, which is more likely to be caused by humanity.

            1.1.2.2.2.1.2. Con:
            There is a very reasonable correlation between CO₂ and global temperatures. Variations in the T trace are due to non-CO₂ factors: solar, ocean currents, volcanoes, and aerosols (p. 1 [36]).

            1.1.2.2.2.1.3. Pro:
            Projected warming from rising CO₂ levels from past IPCC reports has not matched [37] actual temperature increases - projected warming is much higher.

          1.1.2.2.2.2. Con:
          Predictions of past climate models were accurate [38] and may indicate that the science is more reliable than previously communicated.

            1.1.2.2.2.2.1. Pro:
            The projected warming of the combined atmosphere-ocean system has been very accurate (p. 1 [39]).

            1.1.2.2.2.2.2. Con:
            The accuracy of past climate models is nearly impossible to assess because there are few accurate records of past conditions.

              1.1.2.2.2.2.2.1. Pro:
              Limited records exist for aerosol depth [40] prior to the invention of satellites [41] - an important component of determining historical atmospheric behaviour (p. 2 [42]).

              1.1.2.2.2.2.2.2. Pro:
              Historic deep ocean temperature records are poor and only stretch back a few decades (p. 1494 [43]). This limits scientific knowledge on an important heat sink.

              1.1.2.2.2.2.2.3. Con:
              Multiple proxies for past climatic conditions can be used to assess historic trends, including ice cores [44], coral growth [45], stalactites [46], pollen [47], and tree rings [48].

            1.1.2.2.2.2.3. Pro:
            One of the earliest climate models estimated a warming of 0.6°C until 2000 and an atmospheric CO₂ concentration between 375-400 ppm (pp. 24, 26 [49]). The actual CO₂ concentration [50] and the 5-year average temperature [51] were close enough to make this a highly accurate prediction.

            1.1.2.2.2.2.4. Con:
            The projected rate of warming is about 30% higher [52] than what was observed between the years 1970-2016.

          1.1.2.2.2.3. Con:
          The models [53] that are doing the best job of matching ongoing observations are also the ones that predict greater Equilibrium Climate Sensitivity (ECS). This supports IPCC projections, and may encourage less conservative estimates.

          1.1.2.2.2.4. Pro:
          The scientific community has produced a wide range of Equilibrium Climate Sensitivities (ECSs) for the doubling of CO₂, ranging from 1.5 to 4.5°C [54].

            1.1.2.2.2.4.1. Con:
            The IPCC's projections have either been quite accurate [55] or, in some cases, too conservative.

            1.1.2.2.2.4.2. Pro:
            Current satellite temperature records indicate a warming rate of 0.15 to 0.20°C per decade [56]. This further undermines ECS estimates.

          1.1.2.2.2.5. Con:
          Increasing evidence is producing more tightly bounded estimates of Equilibrium Climate Sensitivity, and the effects of aerosols (p. 11 [57]).

        1.1.2.2.3. Con:
        The vast majority [58] of climatology scientists agree that humans caused the recent climate change.

          1.1.2.2.3.1. Pro:
          Even when cross-referencing seven separate studies, each using different methods, there is between a 91-100% [59] consensus agreement that global warming is and has been significantly influenced by humans.

          1.1.2.2.3.2. Pro:
          Support for anthropogenic global warming (AGW [60]) is highest among climatologists with the greatest expertise (p. 1 [61]).

          1.1.2.2.3.3. Con:
          Scientific consensus can be manufactured. Specifically, gatekeepers, such as journal reviewers and editors, have been proven to dismiss and reject papers [62] that argue that climate change is natural, rather than human made, thereby creating a facade of ideological unity.

        1.1.2.2.4. Pro:
        Human-caused climate change is confirmed by many lines of evidence including measurements of outgoing [63] and downwelling [64] infrared radiation (matching predictions of physics) as well as telltale patterns in the vertical human fingerprint of stratospheric cooling with tropospheric warming [65]

      1.1.2.3. Pro:
      Some scientists believe that the current magnitude of climate change isn't unusual compared to historical patterns.

        1.1.2.3.1. Con:
        Historic global warming has never been as rapid [66] as the current shift in temperature.

          1.1.2.3.1.1. Pro:
          Prior to 1950 [67], atmospheric CO₂ levels had not risen above 300 parts per million (ppm) for at least 400,000 years. They have since reached 400 ppm.

          1.1.2.3.1.2. Con:
          Temperatures have only increased approximately 0.85°C in 130 years (p. 2 [68]). While there has been a change - much of which could be attributed to humankind - it isn't significant.

          1.1.2.3.1.3. Con:
          Measures of global warming often use a pre-industrial baseline. However, in the context of the last 10,000 years, those decades were especially cold, meaning that temperature increases may be inflated (p. 1 [69]).

          1.1.2.3.1.4. Pro:
          In the past century, the Earth has warmed [70] ten times faster than normal; without human intervention to stop it, the projected temperature anomaly [71] is a departure from anything seen in hundreds of millions of years [72].

        1.1.2.3.2. Pro:
        The Earth's climate has natural cycles of warmer and colder temperatures. Recent warming is expected, as the Earth may currently be between two ice ages [73], and, as such, would be within a warmer period of the climate cycle.

          1.1.2.3.2.1. Pro:
          The world's ice core temperature and carbon dioxide levels have fluctuated regularly [74] throughout the past 800,000 years.

          1.1.2.3.2.2. Con:
          Recent climate change is largely due to anthropogenic emissions [75] of non-biogenic greenhouse gases through deforestation and the burning of fossil fuels [76]. Heating due to being between ice ages is unlikely to help this.

          1.1.2.3.2.3. Con:
          Recent shifts in climate do not follow, even closely [77], the previous natural cycles of climate change and cannot be correlated to typical contributors like solar output [78] or ocean temperature variability (p. 5 [79]).

        1.1.2.3.3. Con:
        More extreme weather and climate events have been observed since the 1950s [80].

          1.1.2.3.3.1. Pro:
          Temperatures in France have reached 45.9°C [81], which has never happened before.

          1.1.2.3.3.2. Con:
          Changes in weather events since the 1950s have coincided with advances [82] in recording and news distribution. Thus, it may be that we have become more aware of climatic patterns and events, rather than that they have worsened.

          1.1.2.3.3.3. Pro:
          Recently, numerous heat records have been broken and hundreds of floods caused by heavy rains. These events are unprecendented [83] in human history.

        1.1.2.3.4. Pro:
        The suggested temperatures of the Medieval Climate Anomaly indicate that the Earth then was as warm as, or warmer, than the current climate. This brings into question the causes, severity, and necessity of responding to modern climate warming (p. 1625 [84]).

          1.1.2.3.4.1. Con:
          Temperature reconstructions show that there was no globally synchronous warming [85] during the medieval period.

      1.1.2.4. Con:
      There are rare/isolated outlier opinions on any topic – when such views are contradicted by existing evidence (as assessed via scientific methodology) there is no reason for such opinions to influence decision-making.

    1.1.3. Con:
    97% [86] of climate scientists agree on the human origin of the greenhouse gases that contribute to climate change.

      1.1.3.1. Con:
      Much of the analysis that purports to demonstrate the environmental impact of climate change comes from the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC [87]), but they cannot be considered an independent source of information.

        1.1.3.1.1. Pro:
        Findings published by the IPCC may be politically biased by government funding [88] from those that already subscribe to AGW theories. This goes against the principles of skeptical reasoning that underpin good scientific investigation.

          1.1.3.1.1.1. Con:
          Hundreds of experts are involved in the IPCC on a voluntary basis [89] - this large number is likely to guarantee objectivity.

          1.1.3.1.1.2. Pro:
          The US provides a significant portion of the IPCC's budget (p. 5 [90]). Given that the majority of the US typically supports [91] AGW theories, this may may pressure goverment policy to invest in global action and research against clmate change.

          1.1.3.1.1.3. Con:
          Providing policy input based on science is not necessarily political. The appropriate policy responses to the facts provided by the IPCC are left to governments.

          1.1.3.1.1.4. Pro:
          Rajendra Pachauri, the former IPCC Chairman, has repeatedly made false claims about climate change.

            1.1.3.1.1.4.1. Con:
            Pachauri's often mentioned Himalayan Glaciers mistake may have been a typo [92], and was revised after criticism (p. 596 [93]).

            1.1.3.1.1.4.2. Pro:
            Pachauri [94] falsely claimed that the IPCC lead authors and contributors are the 'top of the field' though they are known to hire graduate students.

            1.1.3.1.1.4.3. Pro:
            Pachauri claimed that the Himalayan glaciers [95] would melt by 2035. Such alarmist claims undermine the authority of the IPCC.

              1.1.3.1.1.4.3.1. Con:
              Pachauri's error was highlighted by Graham Cogley [96], who was a contributor to the IPCC report. As this was corrected, it is possible to be confident in the conclusions of the report.

          1.1.3.1.1.5. Con:
          Dominant [97] political forces in the world are opposed to any meaningful action against climate change, lobbying [98] governments to ignore the IPCC's recommendations.

        1.1.3.1.2. Con:
        The scientific community [99] agrees with the IPCC and accepts it as a reliable source.

          1.1.3.1.2.1. Con:
          Since scientists can be both wrong [100] in their findings and incognisant of the mechanisms of making, we should not make policy decisions solely based on what they advocate. This is especially true, given it is so expensive [101] to fight climate change.

            1.1.3.1.2.1.1. Con:
            Scientists needn't fully understand policy making, as political constraints should not be taken into account when humanity's future is at stake.

            1.1.3.1.2.1.2. Pro:
            Fighting climate change requires society-wide cooperation. Cooperation on this scale is inherently political and is thus constrained. It should be handled by policy advisors to achieve maximal efficacy.

            1.1.3.1.2.1.3. Con:
            It is more prudent to trust scientists who are examine and inform the public about the harmful side effects of technologies, than to trust policy advisors, whose economic and political decision making is plagued by issues such as conflicts of interest.

            1.1.3.1.2.1.4. Con:
            Policies are often formed in conjunction [102] with think tanks. Suggestions are thus framed with an awareness of costs and necessity.

            1.1.3.1.2.1.5. Pro:
            Solutions to short-termist thinking require psychological and political approaches that have relatively little to do with climate science. These cannot be commented upon by climatologists.

          1.1.3.1.2.2. Pro:
          It is a myth that dissenting scientists have been vilified, or even that that are dissenting opinions [103].

            1.1.3.1.2.2.1. Con:
            Some claim that Judith Curry [104], a previously widely acclaimed climate scientist, was vilified for her climate skeptical views, leading to the end of her scientific career.

            1.1.3.1.2.2.2. Pro:
            Though historically some scientists may have been illegitimately ostracised, one or two examples do not discredit the whole scientific community.

          1.1.3.1.2.3. Pro:
          Peer review of data [105] is increasingly becoming the norm; the process [106] occurs after data is gathered and may increase the reliability of the data as mistakes could be caught and the paper rejected [107].

            1.1.3.1.2.3.1. Pro:
            In a survey of 928 papers on climate change, no rejection of AGW was found (p. 1686 [108]). Such consensus would not be achieved if the peer review process found erroneous data.

        1.1.3.1.3. Con:
        Scientists are unlikely to be affected by groupthink [109], as they have high incentives to report accurate results.

          1.1.3.1.3.1. Con:
          Though irrefutable evidence can be accommodated by the IPCC, it cannot challenge the base premise of AGW. This is because it is its remit to explain, not refute, AGW.

          1.1.3.1.3.2. Pro:
          Scientific breakthroughs that challenge the status quo [110] are highly rewarded within the scientific community.

          1.1.3.1.3.3. Pro:
          Any results that debunk current reports used by the IPCC will be integrated in the future as they include an extensive literature base [111].

      1.1.3.2. Pro:
      15,000 scientists co-signed an article in 2017 calling for an extreme and aggressive effort to slow down or reverse the trend of climate change before it's too late (p. 1028 [112]). Such action demonstrates the certainty and seriousness of their belief.

        1.1.3.2.1. Con:
        In 2006, 31,487 [113] American scientists signed a similar petition expressing climate skepticism.

          1.1.3.2.1.1. Pro:
          The Oregon petition is the largest attempt [114] to challenge the assumed scientific concensus on climate change.

          1.1.3.2.1.2. Con:
          Of the many scientists who were alleged to have signed a petition expressing climate skeptism, many could not be validated, were clearly fictional [115], or lacked climate expertise [116].

          1.1.3.2.1.3. Con:
          The Oregon petition is over two decades old; scientific consensus has changed [117] a lot in that time.

        1.1.3.2.2. Pro:
        The 2017 petition was a second plea to humanity, after a 1992 article was signed by over 1700 scientists warning [118] of the dangers of ignoring the threat of climate change.

      1.1.3.3. Con:
      Social pressure and research funding incentives have made it difficult for climate scientists to speak out against human-caused climate change claims. As such, claims on the consensus of climate change should be doubted.

        1.1.3.3.1. Con:
        Scientists have been warning society about climate change for over 25 years [119] while facing strong pressure [120] from groups that deny their findings.

        1.1.3.3.2. Pro:
        Scientific practice in an academic environment creates political influence and incentives - these may deter climate change skepticism.

          1.1.3.3.2.1. Con:
          A scientist must report their results accurately [121] if they want to keep their job, no matter the social pressure.

            1.1.3.3.2.1.1. Con:
            Scientists are often given the benefit of the doubt when their results are disputed - this is because of a general trust in their ability to perform experiments. This means that they are not necessarily fired if they inaccurately report their results or make mistakes (p. 2 [122]).

          1.1.3.3.2.2. Pro:
          Knowledge sharing in science, e.g. peer-review publication, is a socially mediated process that is influenced [123] by the current political climate.

          1.1.3.3.2.3. Con:
          The market capitalization of the fossil fuel industry is around $5 trillion [124], and climate change puts this value at risk; there should be plenty of funding for scientists who can disprove climate change and preserve this value.

        1.1.3.3.3. Con:
        Due to sensationalist [125] clickbait journalism, a scientist that could present concrete evidence against climate change would be readily publicised (p. 2 [126]).

          1.1.3.3.3.1. Pro:
          Scientists often make their names from paradigm-disrupting results [127] which perpetuate the myth of scientific progress (p. 5 [128]). As such, scrutiny is applied to many areas of scientific consensus, including climate change, in order to advance humanity.

      1.1.3.4. Pro:
      Accurate measures of the increase in Earth's temperature are correlated [129] with recent increases in CO₂ levels.

        1.1.3.4.1. Con:
        CO₂ is not the only gas that contributes to global temperature increases. Methane [130], produced in abundance naturally, traps heat far better than CO₂, even if it only lasts a decade in the atmosphere.

        1.1.3.4.2. Pro:
        Human CO₂ production [131] exceeds the natural absorption of the global ecosystem by ~15 gigatonnes per year. This was enough to increase the CO₂ in the atmosphere by 100ppm over the last 120 years. This change normally takes 5,000 to 20,000 years to occur.

          1.1.3.4.2.1. Pro:
          The ocean only absorbs [132] about 25% of the atmospheric CO₂ created by burning fossil fuels, so most of it remains in the air.

          1.1.3.4.2.2. Con:
          Higher temperatures increase the degassing of oceans [133], and, consequently, increase the levels of CO₂ in the atmosphere. CO₂ increases may be a consequence of temperature increases instead of vice versa.

          1.1.3.4.2.3. Pro:
          Since the industrial revolution, deforestation [134] has driven up global temperatures as forests cannot absorb as much as before.

        1.1.3.4.3. Con:
        Correlation does not imply [135] causation. It is possible that Earth's temperature has risen for reasons other than increased CO₂ levels.

          1.1.3.4.3.1. Pro:
          Clouds (p. 2 [136]), albedo, and other phenomenon exhibit non-linear chaotic behaviour, making it difficult [137] to predict or project climate change (p. 6 [138]).

          1.1.3.4.3.2. Con:
          The correlation between increased global temperatures and atmospheric constituents is strong enough to assume causation as it can be predicted accurately [139] through models, and is consistent with our understanding of such systems (p. 165 [140]).

    1.1.4. Con:
    Regardless of their link to climate change, actions needed to fight it are necessary in their own right. As such, taking action can only be of benefit.

      1.1.4.1. Pro:
      Resources such as fossil fuels are limited, and the accumulation of plastics in the environment will only cause more problems with time. Transition to sustainable energy sources and recyclable materials will, thus, need to happen, even if the effect of this on climate change is a secondary one.

    1.1.5. Con:
    The fact that human greenhouse gases are the dominant cause of modern warming is not heavily (or even significantly) disputed [141]. Where they exist, every developed nation's National Academy of Science as well as associations of physical scientists endorse [142] this.

    1.1.6. Pro:
    Significant uncertainty remains about influential elements of the climate system such as clouds [143].

      1.1.6.1. Con:
      Cloudiness is primarily a function of other physical conditions (temperature, humidity and aerosols), and as a result uncertainty in cloud modeling primarily affects understanding of climate sensitivity [144] rather than understanding of what is causing global warming to happen.

    1.1.7. Con:
    There is a strong and longstanding scientific consensus that the Earth is warming and that human activities are the primary cause [145].

  1.2. Pro:
  Climate change is harmful for humans and nature (p. 67 [146]).

    1.2.1. Pro:
    Climate change is likely to negatively [147] affect human health.

      1.2.1.1. Pro:
      Climate change is projected to exacerbate deficiencies in micronutrients like zinc and selenium, which means people will need to eat more to remain healthy (p. 2848 [148]).

        1.2.1.1.1. Pro:
        Increased atmospheric CO₂ decreases the protein content [149] of crops, thereby increasing sugar content in rapidly growing plants. This is likely to be harmful as it contributes to both mass bee death and human nutrient deficiencies [150].

        1.2.1.1.2. Con:
        In the future, diets could be supplemented with additional vitamins to ensure adequate nutrition, as is already the case in some areas of the world (p. 6 [151]).

        1.2.1.1.3. Con:
        GMO crops are likely to help mitigate [152] the shortfall in nutrition from those that are traditionally cultivated.

      1.2.1.2. Con:
      Human life expectancy [153] has increased in the past few decades. This demonstrates that climate change has not impacted human health significantly.

        1.2.1.2.1. Con:
        There is an increasing [154] incidence of diarrhoea, heat stress, and malnutrition due to rising temperatures.

        1.2.1.2.2. Con:
        Heat waves [155] killed over 6,000 people in India over a period of eight years.

        1.2.1.2.3. Pro:
        Health problems are likely to be alleviated going forward, as more people have access to vaccines [156] through charitable funding.

      1.2.1.3. Pro:
      In the short term, already existing health problems will be exacerbated, and in the long term, ill-health will increase in many regions (p. 713 [157]). This is expected to increase deaths [158].

        1.2.1.3.1. Pro:
        Famine [159] and diseases associated with migration will rise, due to an increase [160] in displacement [161] from climate change.

        1.2.1.3.2. Pro:
        Developing countries with low incomes and weak healthcare infrastructure will bear the brunt [162] of an increased disease burden [163] due to climate change.

        1.2.1.3.3. Con:
        Through their rapid response [164] to COVID-19, global organisations have demonstrated they have the capacity to cope with catastrophic health events, and are likely to learn and improve [165].

        1.2.1.3.4. Con:
        Movements like Effective Altruism [166] directly target negative health events and encourage money to be spent efficiently to maximise life [167]. Given this, the climate change death toll may not be as high as previously estimated.

      1.2.1.4. Con:
      Income and wealth are the main drivers of human health, not the climate.

        1.2.1.4.1. Pro:
        Half of those living on less than $1.90 USD [168] a day are children. Poverty [169] is an urgent problem which needs to be addressed to protect these children's futures.

        1.2.1.4.2. Con:
        Income and wealth are often influenced by the climate; for example, it is not possible to make money from selling crops if they fail to grow.

          1.2.1.4.2.1. Pro:
          Companies want to invest in areas with potential long term yields; this is a higher risk in disaster prone areas as assets are more likely to be destroyed, deterring investment and reducing income (p. 15 [170]).

          1.2.1.4.2.2. Con:
          The American Midwest sees investment [171] despite changing tornado patterns that threaten corporate bottom lines. This shows that, given sufficient development, wealth can be uncoupled from climate.

        1.2.1.4.3. Pro:
        With higher GDP, human health improves (p. 16 [172]). As humanity becomes richer, it will be more resilient to new vulnerabilities from climate change.

    1.2.2. Con:
    Even profound changes to the climate will not negatively impact the natural world as a whole, which has persisted [173] through massive changes to the climate and ecosystems.

      1.2.2.1. Pro:
      The Earth's climate is well within the bounds of the current Holocene [174], a period during which humans have thrived [175]. Though the climate may be changing, it isn't necessarily bad, as The Earth will survive irrespective of shifts in the climate.

        1.2.2.1.1. Con:
        Even if some parts of the Holocene have been warmer than current temperatures, that doesn’t change the danger [176] that is posed to the world presently.

        1.2.2.1.2. Con:
        74% [177] of the world's population is likely to be threatened by deadly heat when, by 2100, global temperatures exceed [178] those of the Holocene.

        1.2.2.1.3. Pro:
        In the near future, weak sun cycles may limit [179] the extent of winter warming in some areas of the world. This may keep the world within the optimal Holocene temperature range.

      1.2.2.2. Con:
      A potential mass extinction would greatly reduce the diversity of complex animal life; intelligent species should strive to preserve life as it is.

        1.2.2.2.1. Pro:
        Future generations will have less biodiversity [180] than there is currently. This is an inherent loss for human beings, and could damage many interconnected [181] systems.

          1.2.2.2.1.1. Pro:
          The melting [182] of the polar ice caps due to increased temperatures is interfering with natural [183] habitats [184].

            1.2.2.2.1.1.1. Con:
            The melting of the ice caps has happened [185] periodically over millions of years as the Earth warms and cools. Such disruptions to ecosystems are nothing new for the Earth.

              1.2.2.2.1.1.1.1. Con:
              While the cycle of melting ice caps has been a regular feature throughout earth's history, their current degradation is happening at a significantly faster rate [186] than ever seen in its previous geological timeline.

            1.2.2.2.1.1.2. Pro:
            Melting ice could trigger a domino effect [187] of extinctions by disrupting food chains.

            1.2.2.2.1.1.3. Pro:
            For some species, ice loss will likely increase [188] population mixing, reducing genetic differentiation, and could promote the mixing of pathogen communities that previously were isolated.

          1.2.2.2.1.2. Pro:
          Climate change may be a factor behind the global decline in insect numbers [189].

            1.2.2.2.1.2.1. Pro:
            Warmer winters may cause some insects to come out of hibernation too quickly, only to starve [190] due to the lack of flowering plants.

            1.2.2.2.1.2.2. Con:
            Light pollution has been suggested as an important, yet overlooked, driver of insect decline (p. 1 [191]). Climate change has occurred previously, meaning that there is precedent for such changes; this is not true for light pollution.

            1.2.2.2.1.2.3. Con:
            Declining insect numbers may decrease [192] the number of pests that blight crops, meaning that climate change is not particularly harmful for humanity.

          1.2.2.2.1.3. Con:
          There is no such thing as inherent worth - things, such as biodiversity, only have worth if people decide they do [193].

            1.2.2.2.1.3.1. Con:
            The existence of many wild species is vital for the survival [194] of humanity. Given that life is the precursor for creating value, these species have inherent value.

            1.2.2.2.1.3.2. Pro:
            Though wasps are pollinators [195] and control [196] insect pests, people often prefer [197] to focus their conservation efforts on bees [198], demonstrating the variability of worth.

          1.2.2.2.1.4. Con:
          Most models of mass biodiversity loss suffer serious weaknesses, as they either over or underestimate the risks (p. 375 [199]).

            1.2.2.2.1.4.1. Con:
            Biodiversity loss has crossed into a zone of uncertainty [200] which indicates an extreme or existential risk. A large portion [201] of species alive today are threatened.

            1.2.2.2.1.4.2. Pro:
            While there may be a steady loss of biodiversity as a result of multiple factors, the claim that climate change is causing a mass extinction is not certain [202].

          1.2.2.2.1.5. Con:
          Most of the species that have ever existed are now extinct, as species disruption has happened consistently [203] throughout history.

            1.2.2.2.1.5.1. Con:
            Climate change is a driving factor [204] in the increased rate of extinction of a number of species.

            1.2.2.2.1.5.2. Pro:
            Past mass extinction events have taken place over a long time [205]. Such long time lines should not be of much concern to present-day humans.

          1.2.2.2.1.6. Pro:
          Acidification [206] from climate change threatens marine ecosystems.

            1.2.2.2.1.6.1. Con:
            A review of the literature indicates that the acidification of the ocean (+/- 0.2pH) is well within the bounds of normality [207] from the perspective of both small and large geological timescales.

              1.2.2.2.1.6.1.1. Pro:
              Time series studies of Antarctic water show large seasonal and inter-annual variation in ocean pH, which has a strong influence on seasonal ice cover (p. 3 [208]).

              1.2.2.2.1.6.1.2. Pro:
              The pH [209] level for corals of the mid-late Holocene in the South China Sea varied significantly by 22.2% to 25.5% [210]. There have been historical changes that are more significant than those currently occurring.

              1.2.2.2.1.6.1.3. Con:
              There has been an unusually large decrease in pH level for the Great Barrier Reef during the past 60 years [211].

            1.2.2.2.1.6.2. Pro:
            Ocean acidification could be catastrophic [212] for the calcifying organisms that play a vital role in the maintenance of biodiversity.

              1.2.2.2.1.6.2.1. Con:
              Studies show that corals are unaffected by pH variations (p. 13219 [213]).

              1.2.2.2.1.6.2.2. Con:
              Ecosystems have adapted to much greater [214] pH changes than those currently occurring due to climate change.

              1.2.2.2.1.6.2.3. Pro:
              Coral growth rates have plummeted 40% [215] since the mid 1970s, due to acidic ocean waters preventing [216] the buildup of CaCO [217]₃.

              1.2.2.2.1.6.2.4. Pro:
              While some corals can regulate internal pH, this doesn't mean that they are unaffected by ocean acidification. In fact, with decreasing pH, corals suffer decreasing skeletal density (p. 1754 [218]).

            1.2.2.2.1.6.3. Con:
            Calcification rates in marine ecosytems are affected by other factors, such as pollution (p. 1 [219]).

            1.2.2.2.1.6.4. Pro:
            Many animals [220] suffer huge impacts from changing sea pH levels. This may have run-on effects on biodiversity [221] and food chains [222].

              1.2.2.2.1.6.4.1. Con:
              In a peer reviewed survey of acidification experts, only 9 of 23 experts agreed to a 0.8 probability or greater that acidification would result in a loss of biodiviersity (p. 728 [223]).

              1.2.2.2.1.6.4.2. Pro:
              Research [224] based on plausible modelling of pH level declines in the next century has shown that most organisms will lose out.

        1.2.2.2.2. Pro:
        Climate change will increase [225] the occurrence of extreme weather events, favoring flooding, storms [226], heatwaves [227], and wildfires - each of these could destabilise or destroy habitats and ecosystems.

          1.2.2.2.2.1. Con:
          Some extreme weather events, such as tornadoes, have declined [228] in frequency despite climate change.

          1.2.2.2.2.2. Pro:
          Climate change contributed [229] up to 3°C to the 2019 record heatwave [230] that swept through Europe.

        1.2.2.2.3. Con:
        Global warming is likely to extend [231] the tropics; these are the most complex and diverse ecosystems on the planet.

          1.2.2.2.3.1. Con:
          Climate change may bring about a new ice age [232].

            1.2.2.2.3.1.1. Pro:
            Warming temperatures may disrupt vital ocean currents [233] that warm cooler regions and make them habitable. As a result, temperatures in the affected regions could significantly drop and trigger an ice age.

              1.2.2.2.3.1.1.1. Pro:
              Studies suggest [234] that a collapse of the Gulf stream once caused a short ice age in the Northern hemisphere, roughly 12,000 years ago.

              1.2.2.2.3.1.1.2. Con:
              The cooling effect of a grand solar minimum would not override [235] the effect of our enhancement of the greenhouse effect. This limits the chances of another ice age.

            1.2.2.2.3.1.2. Con:
            Scientists expect climate change to actually delay the next ice age by an additional 50,000 [236] years. As such, the problems it would pose are of insignificant concern when compared with more imminent threats from global warming.

            1.2.2.2.3.1.3. Con:
            Humans have survived [237] previous ice ages.

              1.2.2.2.3.1.3.1. Con:
              The Little Ice Age [238] led to mass starvation, disease, and poverty plus huge numbers of human casualties and deaths throughout Europe.

              1.2.2.2.3.1.3.2. Pro:
              Humanity could survive [239] an ice age, given that we are more technologically advanced than in the past.

          1.2.2.2.3.2. Con:
          Already existing tropics could become too hot, reducing biodiversity. Extending the tropics is not [240] a good thing.

          1.2.2.2.3.3. Pro:
          The similar results of a number of studies [241] suggest that the Earth's tropics are expanding towards the poles as the planet warms.

    1.2.3. Pro:
    Food production is likely to decrease [242] significantly because of climate change. This reduction in supply, alongside an increase in demand, will increase the price of food significantly, making it inaccessible for many (p. 494 [243]).

      1.2.3.1. Con:
      A greater concentration of CO₂ would be preferable for some life such as plants, which could in turn benefit humanity.

        1.2.3.1.1. Pro:
        Lower CO₂ levels in the atmosphere are suboptimal for plant growth [244], which increases by 30% in greenhouses where CO₂ levels reach 1500 ppm.

          1.2.3.1.1.1. Con:
          Were CO₂ emissions to reach even 1300ppm, far below the 1500ppm needed for optimal plant growth, the planet could see temperature increases [245] between 6 and 15°C. This would be extremely disruptive [246] to Earth's ecosystems.

            1.2.3.1.1.1.1. Pro:
            CO₂ is only one of several factors that can limit plant growth. While some areas could benefit from increased leaf cover, this may be cancelled out by desertification [247] in other areas.

          1.2.3.1.1.2. Pro:
          Factorial simulations with multiple global ecosystem models of three long-term satellite leaf area index show that 70% [248] of the greening effect are attributable to CO₂ fertilization.

        1.2.3.1.2. Con:
        The rate of change [249] of CO₂ concentration is an issue, as it is too fast to allow species to adapt. As such, there may be negative consequences.

          1.2.3.1.2.1. Pro:
          The evolution and expansion of grasslands after the Oligocene climate change event reduced the viable living space for non-grassland species. Humans should not artificially induce a similar course of events (p. 42 [250]).

          1.2.3.1.2.2. Pro:
          Climate change inevitably changes the living conditions in the habitats of many species on Earth. Some species can migrate or adapt, but studies have shown that as many as 15-37% of species will face extinction in the face of climate change (p. 145 [251]).

          1.2.3.1.2.3. Con:
          Some animals are adapting [252] to climate change; this may limit the extent to which food webs [253] are disrupted.

          1.2.3.1.2.4. Con:
          Plants already have adaptations [254] that allow them to cope with an increased CO₂ concentration; this may help to preserve biodiversity as not all food chains are jeopardised.

            1.2.3.1.2.4.1. Con:
            There is research to suggest that elevated [255] CO₂ levels reduce the nutrient content of crops and will harm humanity [256].

            1.2.3.1.2.4.2. Con:
            Not all plant adaptations to increased CO₂ are positive; famine weed is thriving with anthropogenic global warming to the detriment [257] of other plants.

            1.2.3.1.2.4.3. Pro:
            Plants introduced to new areas are often able to thrive [258] in a climate with increased CO₂, displacing lagging native species. This substitution may allow food webs to continue thriving in new forms.

          1.2.3.1.2.5. Pro:
          Modern plants have symbiotic relationships with animals that have adapted to breathing oxygen at current levels (p. 164 [259]). This symbiotic relationship will be harmed by changes to the atmosphere’s composition.

        1.2.3.1.3. Pro:
        Satellite evidence indicates that the globe has become increasingly greener with plant life due to CO₂ fertilisation [260], despite increases in global temperature.

          1.2.3.1.3.1. Con:
          Even if increased warming makes some areas more suitable for some crops, it also brings droughts, storms, pests, and weeds, that would act against the expansion in arable areas (p. 16 [261]).

          1.2.3.1.3.2. Pro:
          Living plant species have historically adapted [262] to a much warmer climate; they successfully adapted throughout the last 100,000 years so should be expected to continue to thrive.

            1.2.3.1.3.2.1. Con:
            Despite potential growth gains from increased CO₂, rice crop harvests are likely to be counteracted by losses due to increases in heat (p. 1 [263]).

            1.2.3.1.3.2.2. Pro:
            With additional help from geneticists [264], plants can increase their heat resistance and yields.

          1.2.3.1.3.3. Pro:
          Higher temperatures would allow for the expansion of crop production into previously unsuitable areas. As previously cold areas become warm, they become suitable [265] for a new range of crops.

          1.2.3.1.3.4. Con:
          Farming infrastructure depends on time, money, and co-operation to be sucessful. Such [266] resources [267] may be in short supply [268] due to climate change, limiting the benefits of global greening.

        1.2.3.1.4. Con:
        Benefits to plant life from increased CO₂ should be balanced against harms to humans.

          1.2.3.1.4.1. Pro:
          Increased levels of CO₂ can reduce [269] strategic thinking (p. 467 [270]). Given this, current increases in CO₂ levels may have negative outcomes for humanity.

          1.2.3.1.4.2. Con:
          Humans currently contribute to considerable harms to plant life through soil erosion [271]; humanity's inability to monitor its behaviour should not continue to harm other forms of life.

      1.2.3.2. Con:
      Governments often unite [272] to come to the aid of countries being devastated by famine.

        1.2.3.2.1. Pro:
        Developing countries are quickly reducing [273] the portion of their population facing undernourishment. This makes future famines less likely as fewer people are vulnerable to shocks in the food supply line.

          1.2.3.2.1.1. Pro:
          Famine deaths [274] have reduced drastically over time. This may make it less likely that climate change causes significant widespread harm.

          1.2.3.2.1.2. Pro:
          On average, crop yields [275] are growing. This offers a greater level of buffer against famine.

          1.2.3.2.1.3. Con:
          Some improvements in water management have been made, but progress is too slow [276] to be effective. This makes nations particularly vulnerable to famine caused by climate change that reduces the water supply [277].

          1.2.3.2.1.4. Con:
          World hunger has increased in recent years, contributed to by climate change (p. 79 [278]).

        1.2.3.2.2. Con:
        Current biodiversity trends are jeopardising the UN's goals [279] to provide food, water, clothing, and shelter to the global poor. They also weaken natural defences against extreme weather events.

        1.2.3.2.3. Pro:
        International organisations [280] often prepare for disasters and co-ordinate responses between nations; they create interventions with long term benefits, reducing the likelihood of future famines.

          1.2.3.2.3.1. Con:
          A month after the United Nations called for $4.4 billion for famine prevention [281], only 10% of necessary funds had been mobilised. This demonstrates that much effort must be expended to effectively tackle hunger.

          1.2.3.2.3.2. Pro:
          In the 1980s, Ethiopia's famine triggered an unprecedented international response [282]. A similar response alongside good planning may massively aid any nation struggling today.

        1.2.3.2.4. Con:
        Rising temperatures will make some land uninhabitable [283], due to rising ocean levels [284] and desertification. This will impact the quantity of arable land globally and could disrupt the provision of additional food to struggling nations.

      1.2.3.3. Pro:
      Scientists' expectation is that climate change will make existing agricultural practices less effective [285]. This may cause some nations to struggle to adapt rapidly enough to maintain a suitable supply of foods.

        1.2.3.3.1. Pro:
        Countries that rely on food imports [286] could face real difficulties feeding their populations as they struggle to produce their own food and may struggle to buy more in a time of both worsening global security and decreasing global food supply.

        1.2.3.3.2. Pro:
        Future harvests are likely to be negatively affected by water stress [287].

        1.2.3.3.3. Pro:
        Irrigation systems - which will become increasingly important due to diminished and unreliable rainfall - are not affordable for some poor farmers (p. 16 [288]).

        1.2.3.3.4. Con:
        Food insecurity can be minimised by using vertical stack farming. [289]

          1.2.3.3.4.1. Con:
          Several important food crops, like rice (p. 1 [290]), use the C3 metabolic pathway [291], which breaks down at higher temperatures [292]. This may make vertical stack farming inefficient, as it requires lots of energy [293] to maintain ideal growing temperatures.

          1.2.3.3.4.2. Pro:
          Vertical farming [294] may be able to fill shortfalls in agricultural production as it is very spatially efficient [295].

            1.2.3.3.4.2.1. Con:
            Vertical stack farming is highly inefficient [296] with regards to its energy use.

              1.2.3.3.4.2.1.1. Pro:
              Vertical farming may not scale [297] effectively, meaning that complex systems cannot be incorporated.

              1.2.3.3.4.2.1.2. Con:
              Much of the energy used by vertical stack farming could be generated through renewable resources [298].

            1.2.3.3.4.2.2. Pro:
            At their most efficient, vertical stack farms can produce "350 times more food per square yard [299]" than soil based farming.

          1.2.3.3.4.3. Con:
          There is no incentive to invest in vertical stack farming as the technology used depreciates [300] over time, making it a less valuable investment than farm land.

            1.2.3.3.4.3.1. Con:
            Farmland is likely to be less of a long term sustainable investment with unstable future weather patterns [301].

            1.2.3.3.4.3.2. Con:
            The amount of arable land is ultimately fixed in quantity. This forms a natural limit on the ability to invest in it.

            1.2.3.3.4.3.3. Con:
            A great deal of equipment and other forms of capital are depreciable [302]. This does not make them inherently bad investments.

            1.2.3.3.4.3.4. Pro:
            Vertical stack farming faces issues of pests [303] when set up incorrectly - this decreases its value as an investment as yields cannot be assured.

            1.2.3.3.4.3.5. Pro:
            Regulation may damage [304] the profits of vertical stack farmers in the short term. This undercuts potential profits, reducing the short-term value of the technology.

          1.2.3.3.4.4. Pro:
          Vertical farming both requires less water [305] and is less likely to damage [306] supplies than traditional agricultural techniques, which is important given that there are likely to be future water shortages [307].

        1.2.3.3.5. Con:
        Research is ongoing [308] as to the best methods for maintaining [309] crop yields with climate change; these make it more likely that the market is resilient to climate-related disasters like droughts.

      1.2.3.4. Pro:
      Warmer winters allow pests to flourish, reducing [310] crop yields.

        1.2.3.4.1. Con:
        Other animals thrive when there is an increase [311] in potential prey: this is how the food chain operates. This may keep increases in animal populations in check.

          1.2.3.4.1.1. Con:
          Warmer winters have allowed more southern pine beetles to survive winter and expand their range, wiping out swathes of forests [312] in the process. There has been no compensatory rise in predators to prevent the destruction.

          1.2.3.4.1.2. Pro:
          By encouraging [313] native fauna, pests can be kept [314] in check. This also increases biodiversity and creates more resilient [315] biomes.

        1.2.3.4.2. Pro:
        Changes in weather have enabled insects to expand [316] their migratory routes and establish themselves as pests within a single year.

        1.2.3.4.3. Con:
        With more funding, existing [317] aerial spraying operations that reduce the influence of pests could be greatly bolstered.

          1.2.3.4.3.1. Con:
          Aerial spraying can be harmful [318] for native plants and animals, which is especially dangerous given that the process is hard to control.

          1.2.3.4.3.2. Pro:
          Needle blights [319] and mosquito populations [320] have been successfully controlled with limited, controlled aerial spraying programs.

        1.2.3.4.4. Pro:
        Other methods for removing pests, such as the use of pesticides, may make them unsafe [321] for predators to eat.

  1.3. Pro:
  Climate change is getting worse and will be more difficult to fight in the future.

    1.3.1. Con:
    Future generations will suffer from the huge capital loss wasted on fighting global warming today; it would be better to prioritise long term economic growth.

      1.3.1.1. Con:
      Continued economic growth is infeasible as it is not possible to expand forever in a finite space. A transition to a steady state economy [322] is necessary to act on climate change rapidly.

        1.3.1.1.1. Pro:
        By 2050, climate change will present a dire threat to humanity - this may greatly destabilise societies and threaten economies as currently understood (p. 8 [323]).

          1.3.1.1.1.1. Pro:
          Money has limited worth outside of a supportive social environment, as it relies on trust to be a store of value [324]. Climate change will remove that social environment if allowed to continue, making money less useful.

          1.3.1.1.1.2. Con:
          It is unlikely that society will collapse to the extent that money no longer has value or use (p. 6 [325]).

        1.3.1.1.2. Pro:
        Future generations could benefit from a shift away from today's rampant and unsustainable [326] consumerism, as greater access to consumer goods does not necessarily [327] lead to more happiness.

        1.3.1.1.3. Con:
        Degrowth transitions are unnecessary [328] as proven by rich countries; their economies have maintained strength whilst still reducing emissions.

      1.3.1.2. Pro:
      The Copenhagen Consensus Centre [329] has produced a global ranking of the projects that can do the most good for the least amount of money. There are many projects with far higher financial efficiency than climate change.

        1.3.1.2.1. Con:
        In many cases, the values prioritised by sustainable development and climate change adaptability are in broad alignment with efforts to improve quality of life.

          1.3.1.2.1.1. Pro:
          Air pollution is a major cause [330] of death and illness around the globe. Insofar as air pollution and climate change are linked [331], fighting one is related to fighting the other.

          1.3.1.2.1.2. Pro:
          Money used to fight climate change is also money used to improve [332] health and wellness, happiness, and even economic health.

          1.3.1.2.1.3. Con:
          Fossil fuels are essential [333] for increasing living standards in developing countries. Efforts to restrict the exploitation of fossil fuels may impact these people's quality of life.

        1.3.1.2.2. Pro:
        Remediating climate change is expensive and inefficient compared to [334] programmes that would improve access to sexual health and reproductive care.

        1.3.1.2.3. Con:
        The Copenhagen Consensus has repeatedly emphasised the need for green technology investment [335] to protect the planet from climate change.

        1.3.1.2.4. Pro:
        Deadly pathogenic communicable diseases are a greater threat than climate change.

          1.3.1.2.4.1. Pro:
          Infant mortality [336] and curable diseases [337] do untold damage to millions of people annually and should be dealt with. The money spent on stopping climate change could be highly impactful in this instance (p. 631 [338]).

          1.3.1.2.4.2. Con:
          Climate change has an influence [339] on the transmission of disease. For this reason, we should be funding research on cures as well as efforts to reverse climate change.

            1.3.1.2.4.2.1. Pro:
            By increasing the length of transmission seasons, warmer climates contribute [340] to the spread of infectious, vector-borne diseases, such as malaria [341] and dengue [342] (p. 480 [343]).

            1.3.1.2.4.2.2. Con:
            Improved medical research [344] and delivery [345] may drastically reduce the impact of climate change-related diseases.

          1.3.1.2.4.3. Pro:
          The IMF predicted [346] that there was a 3% loss of income in 2020 alone from COVID.

        1.3.1.2.5. Con:
        Given that ranking the effect of a project is often subjective [347], current ratings of the most useful areas for development should not be taken as final.

      1.3.1.3. Con:
      There are a number of low-cost options to reduce carbon emissions, including reforestation [348] and nuclear power [349].

        1.3.1.3.1. Con:
        Enerygy from new nuclear power plants can cost around three times as much [350] as that from renewable sources.

        1.3.1.3.2. Pro:
        Some measures to reduce greenhouse gas emissions, like building insulation [351] or smart grids [352], will save money [353].

        1.3.1.3.3. Pro:
        Solar and wind power [354] are now cheaper than fossil fuels. That could make fighting climate change into a profitable form of business for energy companies.

        1.3.1.3.4. Con:
        The cost of nuclear power is sufficiently high [355] that it has hindered–and even caused the ceasure of–production in the past.

      1.3.1.4. Pro:
      It is now too late [356] to avert the most disastrous effects of climate change, as the Earth is being damaged at a rate faster than it can be fixed [357]. Humanity should begin to seek alternative courses of action.

        1.3.1.4.1. Pro:
        If humans are unable to stop climate change, resources would be best spent finding alternative habitable locations.

          1.3.1.4.1.1. Pro:
          Stephen Hawking claimed [358] that humans must find other inhabitable planets in 100 years, if they are to survive.

          1.3.1.4.1.2. Con:
          Space exploration is incredibly risky [359], especially when humans are beginning to suffer [360] from climate change now. Immediate action should take precedence over speculation.

        1.3.1.4.2. Pro:
        Some believe that it is too late to act and mitigate climate change's damages, and that humanity should instead start "deep adaptation" (p. 1 [361]).

        1.3.1.4.3. Con:
        Climate inactivism [362] frames climate change as being too difficult to reduce, stifling the ability to make incremental improvements.

      1.3.1.5. Con:
      There is a strong consensus among economists [363] that the costs of inaction on climate change are higher than the costs of action [364], and that immediate, aggressive emissions reductions are economically desirable.

    1.3.2. Pro:
    By investing [365] in solving climate change now, efforts to ameliorate future harms are likely to be less intensive [366].

      1.3.2.1. Pro:
      Climate change is one of the few existential [367] threats [368] that humanity faces that we can limit the harms of; given this, it is sensible to try to fight the issue as much as possible.

        1.3.2.1.1. Pro:
        Humans [369] have a unique ability to act upon probable future events, as well as a sense of right and wrong (p. 9015 [370]). This obliges them to attempt to reduce the likelihood of future suffering or extinction.

          1.3.2.1.1.1. Con:
          The moral obligation to fight climate change is trumped by the need to develop poorer countries.

            1.3.2.1.1.1.1. Pro:
            Developing countries did not cause [371] climate change. As such, they should not suffer in the attempt to alleviate it.

              1.3.2.1.1.1.1.1. Pro:
              Rich countries bear the moral burden to prevent further climate change-related hardships given their responsibility [372] for creating the issue.

              1.3.2.1.1.1.1.2. Con:
              Assisting developing nations in becoming fossil fuel-based economies sets them up for failure, as climate change makes this path unsustainable [373].

            1.3.2.1.1.1.2. Pro:
            The same development that causes global warming often lifts people out of immense suffering [374]. It is illegitimate to make people suffer now to protect people in the future.

              1.3.2.1.1.1.2.1. Pro:
              As there are over 150 years of supply [375] left in fossil fuel reserves, there is ample time for a slow shift to green energy. Acting with haste may be unnecessary.

                1.3.2.1.1.1.2.1.1. Pro:
                The continued melting of the polar ice will create new trade routes [376] and provide humanity access to natural resources in the area.

                  1.3.2.1.1.1.2.1.1.1. Pro:
                  The journey time from Europe to Asia will be reduced by two weeks [377] once the Arctic shipping route is navigable for large vessels. This could offer new chances for human advancement.

                  1.3.2.1.1.1.2.1.1.2. Con:
                  The melting Arctic may lead to a scramble for power [378] in the region, potential military squirmishes, and combative behaviour. This decreases the utility of natural resources found there.

                1.3.2.1.1.1.2.1.2. Pro:
                Liberal use of carbon-emitting sources of wealth could improve [379] life in developing nations by making useful products more plentiful.

                1.3.2.1.1.1.2.1.3. Con:
                The price of fossil fuels will rise exponentially as they become scarce and renewable energy sources become cheaper [380]. Transitioning to renewable energy sources will prove to be cheaper in the long run.

                1.3.2.1.1.1.2.1.4. Con:
                Many green energy solutions require high expenditure and long term time investment to innovate [381] and make fully efficient; focussing on continuing fossil fuel extraction may preclude this.

              1.3.2.1.1.1.2.2. Con:
              It is possible to develop without damaging the environment.

                1.3.2.1.1.1.2.2.1. Pro:
                Energy storage technologies [382] can cover the intermittency challenges faced by solar panels. This undermines the advantages of fossil fuels.

                1.3.2.1.1.1.2.2.2. Con:
                Something being possible does not mean that it is easy. Diesel and petroleum generators cost a fraction [383] of the initial cost of solar panels and can be operated 24/7 which is preferable for helping more people quickly.

                  1.3.2.1.1.1.2.2.2.1. Con:
                  The World Health Organisation declared diesel exhaust a carcinogen [384] - a cause of lung cancer in the same category as asbestos and mustard gas.

                  1.3.2.1.1.1.2.2.2.2. Pro:
                  Like diesel, batteries [385] for green technology contribute to environmental and social harms. If neither of them are without side-effects, it is preferable to use the cheaper technology.

                1.3.2.1.1.1.2.2.3. Pro:
                The EnDev project [386] in Ghana offered solar irrigation systems to rural communities that could not connect to the power grid, filling a vital technological gap (p. 10 [387]).

            1.3.2.1.1.1.3. Con:
            The obligations to fight climate change and to develop poorer nations are not mutually exclusive, since developing countries will be severely damaged if climate change continues as projected (pp. 311-312 [388]).

          1.3.2.1.1.2. Con:
          There is no ethical obligation to prevent the extinction of a species as they do not have a moral concept of extinction. Preventing extinction serves purely human beliefs.

          1.3.2.1.1.3. Pro:
          While there may be no universal ethical obligation to prevent extinction, this obligation definitely exists in cases where the extinction is caused by human influence.

          1.3.2.1.1.4. Pro:
          Future people have no ability to advocate for themselves. As such, governments should act in such a way that maximises their future choices. This is not possible in a world with rampant global warming.

            1.3.2.1.1.4.1. Con:
            Presuming that people do not have access to objective morality and that moral standards change [389] over time, there is no reason to believe future people will make decisions people today think are moral. Therefore, we should not faciliate their possible poor behaviour.

            1.3.2.1.1.4.2. Pro:
            Behind a veil of ignorance [390], people would generally agree that it is good to limit suffering and allow people the autonomy to live a good life. This is limited by climate change so people should act against it.

        1.3.2.1.2. Con:
        -> See 1.3.1.4.3.

        1.3.2.1.3. Pro:
        Humans accelerated [391] climate change such that it now worsens [392] by the day and may soon be impossible to stop. Any inaction allows massive harm.

          1.3.2.1.3.1. Pro:
          Lots of social problems are directly or indirectly linked [393] to climate change. As such, the consequences or causes of those issues can be intensified should nothing change.

            1.3.2.1.3.1.1. Con:
            Global political will is consumed by climate change. Given how political systems are traditionally structured, this focus reduces [394] the system bandwith for other issues.

              1.3.2.1.3.1.1.1. Pro:
              There is an opportunity cost [395] for considering one bill over another.

              1.3.2.1.3.1.1.2. Con:
              For any chronic issue, it is usually more efficient to deal with the causes [396] than manage the consequences.

              1.3.2.1.3.1.1.3. Con:
              Even if political will is spent on climate change, there are many other kinds of resources (financial, innovation, etc) that can be used to remain productive elsewhere.

            1.3.2.1.3.1.2. Pro:
            Environmental peace building can smoothen the effects of climate change on conflict (p. xii [397]).

            1.3.2.1.3.1.3. Pro:
            Access to energy has proven to be a good way to tackle many social problems, such as poverty, [398] famine [399], and disease (p. 250 [400]). Climate change is likely to jeopardise [401] this progress as global poverty will be significantly affected [402] by global warming.

          1.3.2.1.3.2. Con:
          Humankind has shown its resilience [403] and will most likely survive [404] climate change. It is better to focus on reducing other extinction risks (pp. 2-3 [405]).

            1.3.2.1.3.2.1. Pro:
            Nuclear war is a greater [406] and more imminent [407] threat than climate change.

            1.3.2.1.3.2.2. Pro:
            Climate change is already very real [408] for many people, so it will continue to be tackled regardless of political action. Governments should now shift toward emphasising other existential threats in order to counteract availability biases [409].

            1.3.2.1.3.2.3. Con:
            Extinction events like an AI singularity can be avoided [410] through sensitive technological deployment.

            1.3.2.1.3.2.4. Con:
            Extinction level events may be predictable [411] with simulation technology - such insights would allow them to be planned for while climate change is dealt with.

      1.3.2.2. Pro:
      Positive feedback loops caused by human-induced changes may take us to a point of no return with more self-reinforcing and accelerated warming, if action is not taken (p. 1 [412]).

        1.3.2.2.1. Pro:
        Some studies [413] suggest that global feedback effects due to cloud cover are likely positive and will increase warming (p. 1 [414]).

        1.3.2.2.2. Pro:
        As snow and ice melt, they reflect [415] less solar radiation, which, alongside the emissions of the melting permafrost [416], causes more heating. This is a positive feedback loop that may cause great harm to humanity.

        1.3.2.2.3. Con:
        There is little [417] that humans can do to address the current feedback loops [418] that are both exacerbated by and exacerbating climate change.

      1.3.2.3. Con:
      Given that money for investment is limited, prioritising adaption over prevention may make the world more resilient [419] should humanity reach the point of no return [420] with climate change.

        1.3.2.3.1. Pro:
        Adaptive strategies like restoring wetlands and floodplains can increase [421] water storage capacities, while also providing a refuge for species. This helps combat two [422] major [423] issues associated with climate change.

        1.3.2.3.2. Con:
        People may struggle [424] to adapt to disrupted ecosystems and adverse climate events, making prevention more important.

          1.3.2.3.2.1. Con:
          Humans have adapted to live in most parts [425] of the world, including deserts [426].

          1.3.2.3.2.2. Pro:
          Climate change drives up wet bulb [427] temperatures everywhere, reducing the number of habitable areas. Such an increase is hard to adapt to effectively (p. 9554 [428]).

          1.3.2.3.2.3. Pro:
          Recovery after major weather events can take a long time; it is better to reduce the need for this process than to attempt to improve adaptation after (p. 2 [429]).

        1.3.2.3.3. Pro:
        Given that some harm from climate change will arise [430], adaptation is a necessity that should not be deferred.

          1.3.2.3.3.1. Pro:
          Even if carbon release was immediately reduced to and sustained at the levels of 2000, this would still be sufficient to raise [431] temperatures by at least 0.5°C over the next two hundred years.

          1.3.2.3.3.2. Pro:
          Acknowledging that sea levels are rising and are likely to continue doing so, affected nations like Bangladesh are innovating new farming methods to maintain [432] their food supply.

          1.3.2.3.3.3. Con:
          Some climate change adaptations do more harm than good [433], contributing to worse outcomes later down the line.

    1.3.3. Pro:
    The cost of fighting climate change will only grow with time. It would be less expensive to deal with it now (p. 1 [434]).

      1.3.3.1. Pro:
      A number of economists [435] support steps to battle climate change, as the costs [436] of fighting climate change are smaller than the damages it causes.

        1.3.3.1.1. Con:
        The insurance industry, which is reliant on economic forecasting, does not view climate change as a serious issue. It has invested over 15 trillion [437] in assets, many of which are not low carbon.

          1.3.3.1.1.1. Pro:
          Portfolio managers are rational [438] and driven by data [439]. If they do not believe that climate change will negatively impact the market, then that may actually be the case.

            1.3.3.1.1.1.1. Con:
            Neither data nor data analysts [440] are free from bias or irrationality. Thus, the conclusions reached by portfolio managers may reflect their personal opinions on climate change impacts, rather than reality.

          1.3.3.1.1.2. Con:
          Growing numbers of insurance firms are divesting from fossil fuels. [441]

          1.3.3.1.1.3. Con:
          Global supply chains may be disrupted [442] if climate change continues. This is likely to be against business interests as it decreases stability.

        1.3.3.1.2. Pro:
        Solar and wind devices can be installed in remote areas to relieve population pressure on cities, while also creating jobs, so the benefit is environmentally multiplied (pp. 8, 22 [443]).

        1.3.3.1.3. Pro:
        Shifting to a circular economy [444] - one that restores and regenerates by design - offers billions of dollars [445] of savings whilst reducing global warming.

        1.3.3.1.4. Con:
        The cost to reduce greenhouse gas emissions is expected to be US$200 billion per annum [446]. This is prohibitively high to the extent that other avenues of action should be sought.

          1.3.3.1.4.1. Con:
          The Earth's GDP in 2020 was almost $85 trillion. [447] The quoted cost of $200 billion per annum [448] is a tiny fraction of this, so the cost is low.

      1.3.3.2. Con:
      Climate change cannot be reversed in the near future without collapsing [449] the economy, which would cause extensive human suffering.

        1.3.3.2.1. Pro:
        The harnessing of energy responsible for climate change has caused unprecedented [450] human flourishing. Products that result in climate change provide comfort and extend human lives.

          1.3.3.2.1.1. Pro:
          The benefits of continued fossil fuel use for individuals likely outweighs the distributed social cost of carbon (p. 5 [451]).

          1.3.3.2.1.2. Con:
          Fossil fuel benefits only outweigh the social costs when it is assumed [452] that the latter are a continuous function over time. Yet, climate science anticipates discontinuities that make the social costs more significant (p. 79 [453]).

          1.3.3.2.1.3. Con:
          Fossil fuels are only beneficial insofar as they are a supply of energy (p. 5 [454]). Improvements to peoples' lives can continue through the use of low pollution sources.

        1.3.3.2.2. Con:
        The current economic system encourages exploitation [455] and inequality [456], leading some to believe that its collapse could mean that it is replaced [457] with a much better system.

        1.3.3.2.3. Con:
        An increasingly wealthy planet may just mean that wealth is concentrated [458] within the hands of a few. It doesn't mean that everyone has the power to adapt to climate change.

      1.3.3.3. Pro:
      Unless action is taken to fight climate change, it will become increasingly economically disruptive and harm commercial activity (p. 4 [459]).

        1.3.3.3.1. Con:
        Fighting climate change is likely to reduce companies' profits. As such, it cannot align with business interests.

          1.3.3.3.1.1. Con:
          Business incentives are often negative [460] and can be ignored, as they are short term. This was demonstrated in the 2007 [461] mortgage-lending collapse.

          1.3.3.3.1.2. Pro:
          A tax on carbon emissions will raise the average cost of energy (p. 1 [462]). This is likely to raise overheads for a number of businesses, stifling innovation [463] as businesses have less money to invest in riskier ventures that could yield technological advancements.

            1.3.3.3.1.2.1. Con:
            By internalizing costs [464] that would otherwise be external, carbon pricing can induce innovation where it is needed and drive forward climate change reduction (p. 5 [465]).

              1.3.3.3.1.2.1.1. Pro:
              Energy-related R&D doesn't crowd out innovation in sectors like the automotive industry (p. 24 [466]). Given this, governments should push to implement carbon taxes.

              1.3.3.3.1.2.1.2. Con:
              Despite much analysis, carbon pricing does not, as of yet, appear to drive innovation (p. 17 [467]).

            1.3.3.3.1.2.2. Pro:
            Increasing the cost of energy through a carbon tax could have negative impacts [468] on agriculture. This would in turn harm food supply, as the industry is highly energy intensive.

              1.3.3.3.1.2.2.1. Con:
              Increasing [469] the costs of carbon intensive foods is likely to nudge consumers toward healthier and less climate-adverse consumption practices.

              1.3.3.3.1.2.2.2. Pro:
              Food insecurity could increase [470] if energy becomes more expensive, harming human health [471] and economic productivity [472].

            1.3.3.3.1.2.3. Pro:
            Carbon taxes may be harmful for both consumers and businesses, as they drive up costs and reduce people's disposable income. This would harm businesses' bottom line as consumers buy less.

              1.3.3.3.1.2.3.1. Con:
              A 2018 report from the New Climate Economy shows that tackling climate change could create USD$26 trillion in economic benefits and save upwards of 700,000 people from premature death by 2030 (pp. 2, 7 [473]).

              1.3.3.3.1.2.3.2. Con:
              Alternatives to carbon taxes, like wealth taxes [474], would not affect the poor negatively and may still accurately assign moral responsibility for emissions.

              1.3.3.3.1.2.3.3. Pro:
              When a government imposes a flat tax on motor fuel, the poor [475] are hardest hit.

              1.3.3.3.1.2.3.4. Con:
              A Robin Hood [476] policy approach was implemented in Australia [477] and deliberately re-directed carbon taxes to help the worse-off. This balances the cost of the tax throughout the population.

              1.3.3.3.1.2.3.5. Pro:
              Initially predicted to cost families €200 a year, increased carbon taxes in the 2018 Irish Budget [478] ended up costing families €500 [479] in 2022, with predictions for further increase. This is unfeasible for lower-income families.

              1.3.3.3.1.2.3.6. Pro:
              In Canada, a carbon tax of CA$50 per ton would translate to an additional 11 cents per liter of gasoline [480] at the pump. This may reduce gas consumption or consumer spending elsewhere.

            1.3.3.3.1.2.4. Con:
            A carbon tax is likely to lead to substantially fewer emissions, as well as long-term [481] positive economic growth.

            1.3.3.3.1.2.5. Con:
            Businesses that can use green energy may be encouraged to transition away from traditional fuels should their overheads [482] increase; they may otherwise put off doing so due to transition costs [483] and path dependencies [484].

          1.3.3.3.1.3. Con:
          Goods may have to be written off [485] from disasters such as floods [486], or tornados [487], if climate change is allowed to continue. This eats into corporate bottom lines as much as anthropogenic global warming mitigation strategies.

          1.3.3.3.1.4. Pro:
          Fighting climate change is a comprehensive process [488] for businesses, involving numerous steps and analyses. Implementing these measures requires direction [489] and human capital [490] which incur expenses.

        1.3.3.3.2. Con:
        Market forces relating to climate change are often distorted [491] by state interventions so they cannot be taken as a clear indicator of the need to fight the problem.

          1.3.3.3.2.1. Con:
          Market forces distort the cost of fossil fuels, as companies benefit from global subsidies equivalent to US$10 million a minute every day [492]. Given this, it is better to opt for the greener energy source.

            1.3.3.3.2.1.1. Con:
            Petrol alone attracts a huge amount [493] of taxation. If the world had no fossil fuel use, there would be a large tax burden which renewables would have to fill.

            1.3.3.3.2.1.2. Pro:
            The International Energy Agency recognises fossil fuel subsidies [494], and measures it at US$440 billion in 2021.

            1.3.3.3.2.1.3. Pro:
            The UK acknowledged that they use fossil fuel subsidies in 2009 as part of the G20 pledge [495] to phase out inefficient fossil fuel subsidies.

            1.3.3.3.2.1.4. Pro:
