            The EU recognises fossil fuel subsidies [496], and allocates an estimated €55 billion in public funding across the EU.

          1.3.3.3.2.2. Pro:
          Many green energy forms receive subsidies [497] that make them artificially cheaper to consumers.

        1.3.3.3.3. Pro:
        The combination of economic losses, damages, and health expenses caused by climate change is projected to cost the US an average of $360 billion a year [498] during the next decade. This is around half of the expected growth of the country's economy.

        1.3.3.3.4. Pro:
        There are fears [499] that the climate crisis could cause a collapse of the global economy. This collapse should be mitigated as much as possible, due to the misery that could ensue.

          1.3.3.3.4.1. Pro:
          Economic harms from climate change would be mitigated in effect for 90% [500] of the world if global warming was limited to 1.5°C.

          1.3.3.3.4.2. Pro:
          The average person [501] will lose 1.3% of their income if the average temperature goes up by 2.5°C - the do-nothing scenario predicted for 30 years from now.

          1.3.3.3.4.3. Con:
          People are already planning ways to maintain the global economy such that major climate change harms are circumvented (p. 4 [502]).

    1.3.4. Con:
    The way societies are organized makes it hard to fight climate change, especially given that it is a problem that spans several generations. It seems unlikely that humanity can significantly reduce this risk.

      1.3.4.1. Pro:
      Fighting climate change may divide humanity if states cannot agree on the best course of action. These disagreements may include who should implement various strategies, and how much each state should contribute financially.

        1.3.4.1.1. Con:
        Climate change and its effects may be more politically divisive than the attempts to fight it are.

          1.3.4.1.1.1. Con:
          Fighting climate change could itself increase pressure on political governance if some countries feel that others are freeloading [503].

          1.3.4.1.1.2. Pro:
          Many military bases are vulnerable [504] to rising sea levels, flooding, drought, desertification, and wildfires. As such, climate change represents a strategic and logistical military threat.

            1.3.4.1.1.2.1. Pro:
            The Pentagon [505] has declared climate change a threat to national security and likely a detriment to military readiness; they believe that it could cause great harm [506], specifically across Africa and the Middle East (p. 2 [507]).

          1.3.4.1.1.3. Con:
          In Europe, populist policy is often nativist [508], meaning that it is highly anti-immigration. This limits sympathy for other nations, which is typically necessary to persuade people to make climate change-limiting sacrifices.

          1.3.4.1.1.4. Con:
          As countries will be asymmetrically [509] affected by climate change, and have great variations in their capacity to mitigate harm, it is unlikely that they will reach a consensus [510] on how to tackle climate change.

          1.3.4.1.1.5. Pro:
          Though states struggle [511] to care for their poorest when implementing climate change remedies, not acting is likely to incur worse outcomes that alter [512] elections or damage low-trust democracies.

        1.3.4.1.2. Con:
        Countries that invest in climate change mitigation may be rewarded economically; this could spur change.

          1.3.4.1.2.1. Pro:
          China's investment in solar technology has allowed them to dominate a large portion of the market [513], and has expanded their opportunities with new related technologies.

          1.3.4.1.2.2. Pro:
          Northern Ireland is seeking to decarbonise [514] its water systems; this could both fight climate change and provide an alternative revenue stream in hydroelectric dams.

          1.3.4.1.2.3. Pro:
          Oil is an industry rapidly decreasing [515] in stability and is forecast to struggle in the future; nations may begin to pivot rapidly towards green tech to stabilise themselves because of this.

          1.3.4.1.2.4. Con:
          The USA was an early investor in green tech yet has seen few returns [516]. Such failures are likely to disincentivize change.

          1.3.4.1.2.5. Con:
          If all countries invest in climate change mitigation technologies, then none would have a competitive advantage [517]. This lack of specialisation [518] may make the project prone to redundancy.

        1.3.4.1.3. Pro:
        Many political systems are currently limited [519] in their ability to take significant action. Those in power benefit [520] from the status quo and fighting [521] climate change decreases [522] their profits.

          1.3.4.1.3.1. Pro:
          Much of Germany's 'Energiewende [523]' is focused on eliminating climate-neutral nuclear power and replacing it with coal and gas power plants.

          1.3.4.1.3.2. Con:
          In order to meet long term goals of reducing greenhouse gas emissions, nations can set interim goals that are easier to achieve. This was done in the Paris Agreement [524].

          1.3.4.1.3.3. Con:
          At a UN summit in 2015, 193 member states adopted 17 sustainable development goals [525] - this demonstrates a clear willingness to move beyond the status quo.

          1.3.4.1.3.4. Pro:
          Any global solution will divide the world against China, as it currently contributes the most towards [526] global emissions and may need to continue [527] doing so, even if through offshored [528] projects.

        1.3.4.1.4. Pro:
        The Global South has seen little benefit [529] from historic pollution but would stand to benefit from its continuation. It is unfair that the Global North would deny them this advancement.

          1.3.4.1.4.1. Con:
          The costs [530] of climate change are borne disproportionately by poorer countries. This negates any benefits they may gain from continuing their current rates of emissions.

          1.3.4.1.4.2. Con:
          Continued emissions from the Global South shift their moral burden [531] to future generations as the Global North reduces their contributions. It is reductive to think of anthropogenic global warming as a quid pro quo [532], so all nations should act imminently.

          1.3.4.1.4.3. Pro:
          The Global North - particularly nations like Britain - actively underdeveloped [533] continents like Africa for their own gain. Denying the Global South advancement now would continue this trend.

      1.3.4.2. Con:
      Global unified efforts are not necessary; individual action, when cumulated, can create meaningful change.

        1.3.4.2.1. Pro:
        Rich US households are each responsible [534] for 12 metric tons of carbon dioxide emissions per year from driving alone - more than the entire emission for some poorer households in developing nations. If these households reduced their emissions, huge changes could be made.

        1.3.4.2.2. Con:
        Some believe climate science research may have been influenced by politics to favor climate change action. This may make them skeptical of interventions.

          1.3.4.2.2.1. Con:
          Many politicians refuse [535] to implement climate change mitigation measures in order to appease financial donors they have from within polluting industries, especially in the US.

          1.3.4.2.2.2. Con:
          Polls [536] show that people do not know the extent of scientific consensus on climate change. Greater awareness of such scientific unity can form the basis for broader unity.

          1.3.4.2.2.3. Pro:
          A non-trivial [537] number of the global population does not believe in climate change or underestimates [538] its impacts, making political change difficult.

          1.3.4.2.2.4. Pro:
          Pro-environmental individuals are more likely to be politically left-wing (p. 1 [539]). This may skew their approach to climate change action.

        1.3.4.2.3. Con:
        Structural change is likely to be more effective when tackling anthropogenic global warming than individual actions, due to the scale of the issue.

          1.3.4.2.3.1. Con:
          Though unnecessary, many businesses make decisions based on public opinion. Without public acceptance and pressure, it is unlikely [540] that businesses that rely on fossil fuels would switch production.

          1.3.4.2.3.2. Pro:
          US households and businesses, combined, are responsible [541] for over 10% of US GHG emissions. A significant change could be made through national structural investment.

        1.3.4.2.4. Con:
        It is reductive [542] to think of transformative change and individual action as oppositional or binaristic.

        1.3.4.2.5. Pro:
        Vegetarianism can contribute to reducing [543] global CO₂ emissions. If done en masse, swathes of land could be reclaimed for uses that benefit [544] the climate.

      1.3.4.3. Con:
      Humans are capable of changing the way our society is organised. With a focus on long term effects and short term sacrifices, the necessary reorganization of society to battle climate change may be possible.

        1.3.4.3.1. Pro:
        There are already signs that the fight against climate change has achieved some success - this may spur progress forward.

          1.3.4.3.1.1. Pro:
          The hole in the ozone layer is recovering [545], demonstrating humanity's capacity to take meaningful action against global warming.

          1.3.4.3.1.2. Pro:
          Forests are expanding [546] in developed countries, indicating that action against climate change is beginning to take shape.

          1.3.4.3.1.3. Con:
          COP26, held in 2021, offered limited action [547] to combat climate change despite massive pressure from many citizen groups [548] and NGOs [549]. Current successes may be all that humanity can achieve.

        1.3.4.3.2. Pro:
        Multigenerational action is possible. Many large scale religious and cultural projects, such as cathedrals [550], took generations to build.

          1.3.4.3.2.1. Pro:
          Sociologist Max Weber [551] argues that modern society has replaced traditional reliance on charisma with bureaucracy and technocratic management. This allows for longer term projects.

          1.3.4.3.2.2. Con:
          Trump's anti-China populist rhetoric [552] led to an increase in trade barriers [553]. A lack of co-operation on trade indicates that other forms of co-operative progress, like those requiring multigenerational cohesion, may be unlikely.

          1.3.4.3.2.3. Pro:
          Recent social movements are making generational changes that will help to break down [554] patriarchal structures.

        1.3.4.3.3. Con:
        Recent disputes [555] over the Ethiopian dam demonstrate how many nations see energy and water use as zero sum [556]. This disrupts cooperation and makes multigenerational action difficult.

        1.3.4.3.4. Con:
        Capitalism doesn't allow for transformative change in the manner that is necessary for combatting climate change, given that change can threaten corporate profits.

          1.3.4.3.4.1. Con:
          Over 300 global corporations so far have committed to 100% renewable energy [557] - many by as early as 2025. Companies are putting pressure on others to create change.

          1.3.4.3.4.2. Pro:
          Greenwashing scandals [558] are common as companies prioritise [559] profit over ethical behaviour.

          1.3.4.3.4.3. Con:
          Capitalism's destructive impulses can be contained and manipulated [560] through state intervention.

            1.3.4.3.4.3.1. Pro:
            Cap-and-trade systems [561] use a capitalist logic and yet support the regulation of CO₂ emissions. They allow flexible access to resources with those who most desire coal paying a high price to access it. Proceeds can be used to off-set emissions.

            1.3.4.3.4.3.2. Con:
            Many companies are likely to satisfice [562] regulation which would impede intervention and make it difficult to fully curtail capitalist destruction.

    1.3.5. Pro:
    As the globe heats, the risk of triggering more abrupt, destructive and irreversible shifts in climate patterns and local system equilibria – "tipping points" – increases [563].

  1.4. Con:
  Prioritising innovation in climate change adaptation would be better than fighting the issue directly, as direct action on climate change is limited by a lack of innovation in action to prevent it.

    1.4.1. Pro:
    Technological advancement is unpredictable [564], unreliable, and constrained by physical laws. To assume we do not need to adapt to climate change because technology will fix it is unrealistic.

      1.4.1.1. Con:
      Various sustainable development initiatives [565] demonstrate the feasibility of fighting back against climate change.

        1.4.1.1.1. Pro:
        Zero waste strategies [566] are fighting human-induced climate change by slowing down human emissions. Without these strategies, climate change would be a larger threat.

        1.4.1.1.2. Con:
        It may take time to identify whether sustainable development initiatives have been effective in reducing the impact of climate change (p. 1 [567]). Give this, significant investment into adapting to climate change should occur to minimise potential harm.

        1.4.1.1.3. Pro:
        Commercial sustainable development solutions modelled by Project Drawdown are being acted on [568]; these may create change [569] and spur innovation [570].

        1.4.1.1.4. Pro:
        It may be possible to sequester large amounts of carbon by increasing [571] whale populations.

        1.4.1.1.5. Con:
        Sustainable development initiatives are beleaguered with corruption [572] that reduces their efficacy.

      1.4.1.2. Con:
      Developments [573] in artifical intelligence have shown that it may be possible for machines to solve problems independently; this could be extended to provide creative solutions for issues like climate change.

        1.4.1.2.1. Pro:
        Better computing capacity allows us to calculate more complex models with more variables. In the future, we may be more able to take preventive action to halt or limit climate damage, including against adverse weather events [574].

          1.4.1.2.1.1. Con:
          Even if we can predict bad weather events like hurricanes, they will still do extensive damage [575] that could be avoided or minimized by fighting climate change.

          1.4.1.2.1.2. Pro:
          Sophisticated modelling [576] has helped to develop plans for longer term climate action, making it easier for people to visualise the necessary steps for change.

        1.4.1.2.2. Con:
        It has proven difficult to apply AI systems to wider contexts as it is hard to build generalized learning techniques. The technology often has issues [577] with the cross-applicability of knowledge.

      1.4.1.3. Pro:
      Climate change mitigation technology cannot reverse the profound loss of biodiversity [578] caused by climate change, especially as green technologies, like hydroelectric dams, can be damaging (p. 265 [579]).

        1.4.1.3.1. Pro:
        Hydroelectric power [580] produces very large amounts of greenhouse gases when land is dammed and vegetation rots. The worst hydro plants can produce more greenhouse gases [581] than coal plants.

        1.4.1.3.2. Con:
        Though some harms of hydroelectric technology are unavoidable, harms caused by humans [582] are limited as dams only need to be constructed once, and the environment can be safeguarded [583] through prior planning.

        1.4.1.3.3. Pro:
        The construction of dams has an impact on the coast of a country, with the lack of sand reaching the sea causing a rise in the levels of marine abrasion (p. 419 [584]).

        1.4.1.3.4. Con:
        Retaining biodiversity is not a critical part of fighting climate change, as it is a second order effect [585] of the main issue.

        1.4.1.3.5. Con:
        The side effects of green energy production could be mitigated through a number of policy interventions, including monitoring [586].

      1.4.1.4. Pro:
      Scientific progress has slowed [587] such that breakthroughs on how best to fight climate change may now be fewer and farther between.

        1.4.1.4.1. Con:
        Though technological innovations do not proceed [588] linearly, they do still continue to take place.

        1.4.1.4.2. Pro:
        After many decades of research and development, humanity still doesn't have cures for cancers [589], viable nuclear fusion [590], or a foothold on any planet [591] other than the Earth.

        1.4.1.4.3. Pro:
        Scientific progress increasingly takes more brain power [592] than it did in the past, and yields fewer returns. Given breakdowns in scientific collaboration [593], this may make fighting climate change highly difficult.

        1.4.1.4.4. Con:
        Humanity already knows what needs to be done [594] to fight climate change, breakthroughs in science that make this easier, though nice, are unnecessary.

    1.4.2. Pro:
    Technological adaptation may be sufficient to handle any adverse effects caused by climate change.

      1.4.2.1. Con:
      Efforts to fight and adapt to climate change should not be taken as an exclusive binary. We can and should do both.

        1.4.2.1.1. Pro:
        Realistic models of carbon-reduction show that lagging the use of fossil fuels while retooling industrial infrastructures and the economy will lead to [595] future heat growth. As such, adaptation without attempts at solving the issue could lead to more harm.

        1.4.2.1.2. Con:
        Many current adaptation and impact management strategies for climate change also contribute to carbon emissions, e.g. by increasing [596] the need for construction necessary in implementing such strategies (p. 27 [597]).

        1.4.2.1.3. Pro:
        Sponge cities [598] can enable the easy recycling of water, allowing regions to adapt to water shortages whilst also making it more green.

      1.4.2.2. Con:
      Climate change could cause political unrest [599] and violence in poor countries, limiting their capacity for adaptation.

        1.4.2.2.1. Pro:
        Competition for habitable [600] land is likely to increase migration and resource competition that leads to political unrest.

          1.4.2.2.1.1. Pro:
          People will be displaced as rising waters [601] destroy previously habitable land.

            1.4.2.2.1.1.1. Pro:
            Many large cities [602], like New York, Jakarta, and London, will be underwater by 2100 if climate change is not stopped soon.

          1.4.2.2.1.2. Con:
          Habitable or producible land is often reduced through mismanagement, so it can be reclaimed [603] through proper management.

          1.4.2.2.1.3. Pro:
          Climate change may turn existing [604] rainforests into dry savannahs.

          1.4.2.2.1.4. Con:
          Increasingly, dense housing [605] infrastructure is viable and climate friendly. This may mitigate issues of reduced land access, and revitalise [606] vernacular traditions.

          1.4.2.2.1.5. Pro:
          Climate change is likely to make parts of the planet either uninhabitable [607] or too hot [608] for humans to work in, particularly in equatorial zones.

        1.4.2.2.2. Con:
        The causes of migration are interdependent [609] and cannot be easily linked to climate change alone. Adaptation may still be successful in limiting demographic change.

          1.4.2.2.2.1. Con:
          A World Bank report estimates [610] that 140 million people could be forced to migrate due to climate change by 2050.

          1.4.2.2.2.2. Pro:
          Politics and poverty, rather than climate change [611], are to blame for past conflicts and migration issues in East Africa.

            1.4.2.2.2.2.1. Con:
            The main root cause of global warming is resource exploitation, through aggressive extraction [612], consumption [613], and colonialism [614]. This leads to increasing wealth inequality [615] - a contributor to relative poverty.

            1.4.2.2.2.2.2. Pro:
            Extremist groups, driven by a myriad of factors, may increase migration from East Africa through their violence [616]. These are likely to be more immediately threatening than climate change.

          1.4.2.2.2.3. Con:
          Migration from poorer nations is likely to increase, as they are often more exposed [617] to the impacts of climate change and less able to afford adaptation.

            1.4.2.2.2.3.1. Pro:
            Small farmers in Uganda [618] face a decline in their access to fertile land because of extreme weather conditions, which affects their income and forces them to migrate.

            1.4.2.2.2.3.2. Pro:
            Climate change is a major driver for migration [619] from Central America.

            1.4.2.2.2.3.3. Con:
            An expert in modern statistics has written extensively about how absurd [620] it is to link climate change to refugee influxes.

          1.4.2.2.2.4. Pro:
          Many migrate to find work and send remittances [621] to their loved ones in their home country. Climate change may not impact the supply of jobs–either at home or abroad–and so may not influence this.

        1.4.2.2.3. Con:
        The international community is already adapting to increased migration, meaning that political systems are unlikely to be overwhelmed by any changes due to climate change (p. 1 [622]).

          1.4.2.2.3.1. Pro:
          Organisations like the Climate and Migration Coalition [623] are working to improve migration policies, as a way to better adapt to climate change.

          1.4.2.2.3.2. Con:
          Given that existing [624] migration stresses are poorly dealt with, despite their longevity [625], it seems unlikely that the international community's planning for increased climate migration will be sufficient.

          1.4.2.2.3.3. Con:
          Even migration over short distances can greatly change [626] political dynamics in ways that cannot be accounted for. This exacerbates the perils of climate change.

        1.4.2.2.4. Pro:
        Climate change has exacerbated wealth inequality globally, which may lead to unrest. This should drive climate change mitigation policies to reduce harm (p. 9808 [627]).

          1.4.2.2.4.1. Con:
          Having social wealth inequality may not be detrimental, and could even be good. Therefore, this is not a reason to fear climate change.

            1.4.2.2.4.1.1. Pro:
            Innovations create income inequalities at the top level, as inventors and entrepreneurs become rich (p. 41 [628]). Thus, economic inequality is a by-product of economic growth and nothing to be scared of.

            1.4.2.2.4.1.2. Pro:
            Poverty is the problem [629], not inequality. The income divide between someone earning $300,000 and someone earning $3 million is unlikely to cause problems.

              1.4.2.2.4.1.2.1. Con:
              As long as there is scarcity of resources, inequality may correlate with some degree of poverty as the rich use their wealth to meet their demands to the detriment of the poor (p. 4 [630]).

              1.4.2.2.4.1.2.2. Pro:
              Inequality does not create poverty in an absolute sense; people's definitions of poverty change [631] relative to the standards of the time.

              1.4.2.2.4.1.2.3. Con:
              The forces that create inequality often benefit from keeping people impoverished [632]. Minimum wages are kept low, benefits are denied, and wealth is often not distributed.

              1.4.2.2.4.1.2.4. Pro:
              Poverty indicates social ills that may encourage [633] inequality. Nonetheless, the former is more significant as it is likely to impede people's acquisition of the bare necessities of life.

            1.4.2.2.4.1.3. Con:
            Economic inequality is generally assumed [634] to be bad for economic growth, as it reduces educational opportunities.

            1.4.2.2.4.1.4. Con:
            Income inequality is associated [635] with many kinds of social problems, like political turmoil, and health inequality [636].

          1.4.2.2.4.2. Pro:
          Climate change may cause extensive infrastructural damage, leading to political instability and large financial losses (p. 4 [637]). The poor are least able to cope [638] with these damages.

          1.4.2.2.4.3. Pro:
          If climate change disrupts global supply lines and makes food more scarce, price increases are likely to be most keenly felt by the poorest (p. 398 [639]).

      1.4.2.3. Pro:
      Farming technology has contributed to increased crop yields [640]. The trend is not reversing, which may mean that the industry, with reasonable adaptations, is resilient to the effects of climate change.

        1.4.2.3.1. Con:
        GMOs have led to the evolution of superweeds [641] and pests that are able to devastate large populations of crops - this may jeopardise crop yields when paired with the effects of a changing climate.

        1.4.2.3.2. Pro:
        The United States, one of the biggest food producers in the world, uses just over a quarter [642] of its produce to feed people directly. It may not be difficult to shift the balance of crop use if the climate changes.

          1.4.2.3.2.1. Con:
          Over a third [643] of US land and food production is used to feed animals [644] which makes this an intractable land use issue that may inhibit adaptation to climate change.

          1.4.2.3.2.2. Pro:
          Decreased consumption of animals would be an easy way to adapt to climate change food insecurity, especially given the amount of food [645] and water [646] they require per pound of flesh (p. 439 [647]).

        1.4.2.3.3. Con:
        Farming technology may be unable to keep apace with the 70% increase [648] in crops needed to feed the world by 2050, especially given that current efficiency maximising strategies are unlikely to be sufficient to meet demand (pp. 5-6 [649]).

        1.4.2.3.4. Pro:
        Regenerative agriculture [650] may greatly improve farming in the future, making shocks to the system less likely.

      1.4.2.4. Pro:
      Injury and mortality rates associated with climate-linked weather anomalies have dropped drastically over the last 100 years, indicating that technological adaptations could override increases in danger from extreme weather events (p. 102 [651]).

    1.4.3. Con:
    Existing technology is sufficient to fight climate change; innovation is not necessary.

      1.4.3.1. Con:
      Even if green methods to fight climate change are technically feasible, they may not be financially viable. As such, reduction strategies involving them should not be prioritised over adaptation.

        1.4.3.1.1. Con:
        Finance is a tool [652] that facilitates the provision of necessities in a complex society; it can be reoriented to fight climate change.

          1.4.3.1.1.1. Pro:
          The US government and related agencies spent a hundred billion dollars [653] on reprogramming software fearing the Y2K bug. Financial feasibility is not a limiting factor when battling critical issues.

          1.4.3.1.1.2. Pro:
          Even if humans currently lack tools that are either cheap or efficient enough to fight climate change, they should still continue to innovate to reduce costs [654] and make further breakthroughs [655].

            1.4.3.1.1.2.1. Pro:
            Investing in green technology will help humanity break away from existing path dependencies to find technological tipping points that can turn the tide on anthropogenic global warming (p. 955 [656]).

            1.4.3.1.1.2.2. Con:
            A scarcity [657] of green technology materials will likely keep prices high regardless of the innovation that occurs. This may cause significant harm to the poorest in society.

          1.4.3.1.1.3. Con:
          White collar crime is often rampant [658] and hard to clamp down upon; given this, it is hard to believe that finance can effectively be reengineered to fight climate change.

        1.4.3.1.2. Pro:
        Since 2015, approximately $300 billion per year [659] has been spent to reduce annual emissions, yet it is predicted to take until 2050 for the half [660] of the world's energy to come from wind or solar sources.

          1.4.3.1.2.1. Con:
          There is a strong trend [661] toward the increasing use of solar and wind for electricity generation, indicating a willingness to alter the balance of sources in countries' energy mix.

            1.4.3.1.2.1.1. Pro:
            Several countries already generate [662] 100% of their energy from renewable sources, leading the way for other nations to follow suit.

          1.4.3.1.2.2. Con:
          Renewable energies like wind and solar are becoming cheaper [663] per kilowatt. This may encourage increased adoption of these energy generating methods.

            1.4.3.1.2.2.1. Con:
            Wind and solar generated energy does not reflect the cumulative costs of their waste's environmental impact [664].

            1.4.3.1.2.2.2. Pro:
            Offshore wind is now so cheap in some areas of the world that the return on investment may pay back [665] subsidies to governments.

            1.4.3.1.2.2.3. Con:
            While the creation of green energy may be becoming cheaper, storing it still comes at a very high cost [666].

          1.4.3.1.2.3. Pro:
          Some denounce [667] wind and solar expansion as they feel such interventions are eye sores. This may greatly limit the shift toward green technology.

        1.4.3.1.3. Con:
        The economic cost of repairing damages caused by climate change, combined with those necessary for adaptation, may be higher than the costs of prevention alone.

          1.4.3.1.3.1. Pro:
          If climate change continues, buildings will need to be altered [668] to handle the adverse effects. This is likely to become very expensive rapidly.

            1.4.3.1.3.1.1. Pro:
            Tornado proof homes cost around 20-30% more than regular homes (p. 231 [669]). This climate change driven adaptation is too expensive, given that destructive events would still occur and disrupt life.

          1.4.3.1.3.2. Con:
          Hurricane Harvey caused damages of US$125 billion [670] - adapting to climate change could reduce bills like these.

            1.4.3.1.3.2.1. Pro:
            Climate change is thought to increase [671] the frequency and severity of hurricanes. Adapting to the effects of climate change and preparing for such devastation could reduce expenditure in the long run.

          1.4.3.1.3.3. Pro:
          Many countries have set targets [672] of zero greenhouse gas emissions in the near future and are able to keep on pace without hurting their economies. This is vastly preferable to taking drastic action later.

            1.4.3.1.3.3.1. Pro:
            Fighting climate change could create [673] 65 million new jobs globally - a major benefit that would offset any harms from spending.

            1.4.3.1.3.3.2. Con:
            While decoupling economic growth and environmental harm is feasible [674], it requires good governance which corrupt nations may lack at present (p. 1 [675]).

          1.4.3.1.3.4. Con:
          The global fleet of coal plants could be replaced with clean energy and storage, as they will become overwhelmingly uncompetitive with renewable energy sources (p. 9 [676]). In making this change, vast savings could be made and emissions reduced.

      1.4.3.2. Pro:
      Geoengineering [677] shows promise [678] in fighting climate change so should be prioritised over adaptation.

        1.4.3.2.1. Pro:
        Biochar [679] can remove atmospheric carbon sequestered in trees and thereby reduce CO₂ levels (p. 1 [680]).

        1.4.3.2.2. Con:
        Geoengineering is a moral hazard [681]; by ameliorating the perception of immediate threat from rising greenhouse gases, geoengineering removes the incentive to stop emitting additional gases.

          1.4.3.2.2.1. Con:
          Moral hazard only arises if geoengineering works as people can then use it as a fix-all for resource misuse. If geoengineering doesn’t work, humanity will have no option but to reduce their emissions as there are no other alternatives for fighting climate change.

          1.4.3.2.2.2. Pro:
          People may presume that geoengineering allows them to avoid personal culpability for their actions - this creates further harms as it undermines the aims of the technology (p. 1 [682]).

        1.4.3.2.3. Con:
        As the necessity of geoengineering is highly disputed [683], there is little research into its efficacy, which makes it quite unreliable.

          1.4.3.2.3.1. Pro:
          Attempts to side-step a deep-effort solution through atmospheric geoengineering [684] may produce an equivalent reduction [685] in agricultural productivity.

            1.4.3.2.3.1.1. Pro:
            Suggested [686] geoengineering experiments include those modelled on the Mt Pinatubo eruption, which resulted [687] in dimming as well as cooling. Subsequent agricultural losses outweighed gains to production from cooling.

            1.4.3.2.3.1.2. Con:
            A decline in agricultural productivity is an acceptable tradeoff when tackling climate change, as the world already heavily overproduces food [688]. It may be possible to suffer a decline in agricultural productivity without causing harm.

          1.4.3.2.3.2. Pro:
          Fixing global warming through carbon capture [689] might solve temperature problems but will not solve other air toxicity problems.

            1.4.3.2.3.2.1. Pro:
            Complex interactions of chemicals due to climate change might make them more toxic, potentially harming [690] the environment.

            1.4.3.2.3.2.2. Con:
            Melting ice exposes more seawater to the atmosphere, which may make it easier for toxic chemicals in Arctic waters to escape into the air. Global warming could produce more air pollution [691] so should be stopped.

          1.4.3.2.3.3. Con:
          Attempts at geoengineering may further research, increasing scientific understanding of climate change. This would better equip us to predict future impacts and limit their repercussions.

          1.4.3.2.3.4. Pro:
          It might not be possible to research the harms of geoengineering fast enough to mitigate their impact if used unwisely - especially given the complexity of the systems involved (p. 190 [692]).

          1.4.3.2.3.5. Con:
          Though solar geoengineering may have negative consequences, these can be mitigated through social interventions. Such actions would be inexpensive and reversible which makes them attractive (p. 1 [693]).

        1.4.3.2.4. Pro:
        Recent work has suggested the possibility of removing carbon from the ocean [694]; as carbon is more concentrated in the ocean than the atmosphere, removing carbon from there would be more efficient [695].

          1.4.3.2.4.1. Con:
          Removing carbon from the ocean at the scale required to reduce climate change would produce enormous quantities [696] of environmentally damaging solids.

      1.4.3.3. Pro:
      Renewable sources of energy are a viable [697] option for fighting climate change, yet these solutions are often not used due to fossil-fuel company monopolies [698].

        1.4.3.3.1. Pro:
        Ending subsidies [699] for fossil fuels would cut global carbon emissions [700] by 20% and destabilise monopolies as new companies are able to control prices [701].

        1.4.3.3.2. Pro:
        Transitioning to electric cars [702] would cut emissions and greatly reduce the impact of climate change. This process may be slowed by continuing fossil fuel monopolies.

          1.4.3.3.2.1. Pro:
          Car batteries from Tesla are now at the tipping point [703] of being cheaper and more efficient than combustion engines (p. 19 [704]).

          1.4.3.3.2.2. Con:
          The beneficial impact of transitioning to electric vehicles depends on rapid decarbonisation [705] of electricity generation which is, in practice, a drastic measure.

            1.4.3.3.2.2.1. Con:
            In the UK, farmers are swapping [706] crops for solar panels - indicating that there are rapidly changing incentives that promote decarbonisation.

            1.4.3.3.2.2.2. Pro:
            Replacing traditionally fuelled machinery with electric machinery requires resource-intensive battery building, which is both environmentally costly [707] and unsustainable [708].

          1.4.3.3.2.3. Pro:
          At present, electric vehicles in some nations produce a third of the CO₂ [709] of their traditional counterparts; these advantages of reduced CO₂ emissions may only increase given rapid improvements [710] in the technology.

        1.4.3.3.3. Con:
        There are no viable energy sources [711] that have comparable energy density to fossil fuels meaning that it is highly difficult to effectively fight climate change while satiating energy demands.

          1.4.3.3.3.1. Pro:
          Oil resources can be transported conveniently [712], bypassing the need to build other complex systems like hydroelectric dams [713], or solar panel networks and storage systems (p. 209 [714]).

          1.4.3.3.3.2. Con:
          It is worthwhile to use an energy source which is less efficient than fossil fuels if it causes less environmental harm.

          1.4.3.3.3.3. Con:
          It may be feasible [715] to refine biochar into a high density fuel source in the future which could make climate change easier to fight.

        1.4.3.3.4. Con:
        There is no technically feasible replacement, globally, for the vast amounts of fossil fuel used currently within the energy mix, even without monopolies.

          1.4.3.3.4.1. Pro:
          Wind or solar generated energy cannot replace all forms of production, as there is not enough sunlight or wind to consistently to do so.

            1.4.3.3.4.1.1. Con:
            It is only [716] necessary to capture and use a 10,000th of incoming solar radiation to satisfy the world's current energy needs.

              1.4.3.3.4.1.1.1. Pro:
              Excess energy generatied from renewable sources can be stored in power grids [717] to be released later or elsewhere to fill supply/demand gaps.

              1.4.3.3.4.1.1.2. Con:
              The ability to capture solar radiation is limited by a meagre supply of critical building materials like rare earth metals (p. 39 [718]). This may mean that world's energy needs cannot be met through this measure.

            1.4.3.3.4.1.2. Pro:
            Wind and solar generated energies only make up approximately 5.4% of the world's current energy mix. This small percentage of renewables is important as it shows this may not be a feasible climate change reduction strategy (p. 30 [719]).

            1.4.3.3.4.1.3. Con:
            Renewables, though presently a small section of the energy market, are likely to grow [720] in usage with time.

            1.4.3.3.4.1.4. Con:
            Less land [721] is necessary to provide renewable energy than is currently taken up by the fossil fuel industry.

              1.4.3.3.4.1.4.1. Pro:
              Deep-sea wind farms [722] could produce clean and renewable energy, freeing up land for humans to live on.

            1.4.3.3.4.1.5. Pro:
            Even if power could be generated using windfarms, they have harmful effects on wildlife.

              1.4.3.3.4.1.5.1. Con:
              Simple measures, such as painting [723] turbine blades or playing alarms [724] as birds approach, can reduce wildlife death from wind turbines.

              1.4.3.3.4.1.5.2. Pro:
              In the United States, it is estimated that wind turbines kill between 140,000 and 500,000 [725] birds per year, increasing the social cost of the technology.

          1.4.3.3.4.2. Pro:
          Nuclear power is a relatively low carbon technology, but remains contentious [726] due to its perceived dangers [727], making fossil fuels preferable.

            1.4.3.3.4.2.1. Pro:
            Nuclear power is not environmentally clean as the waste [728] is of great concern.

            1.4.3.3.4.2.2. Con:
            Nuclear fission [729] produces little waste, and few emissions; it is a highly desirable alternative to our current energy supplies.

            1.4.3.3.4.2.3. Con:
            Changing policy making based on perceived risk is incredibly reckless given how much safer [730] nuclear power is compared to other forms of energy production.

              1.4.3.3.4.2.3.1. Con:
              Nuclear accidents [731] are a considerable risk that often shape energy policy making. They could negatively influence future generations (p. 1 [732]).

              1.4.3.3.4.2.3.2. Pro:
              Nuclear power production causes significantly fewer [733] deaths than coal.

              1.4.3.3.4.2.3.3. Pro:
              Nuclear power generates [734] less radiation than alternative technologies.

          1.4.3.3.4.3. Con:
          Global investment into renewables stood at USD$750 billion [735] in 2021. This shows that huge strides are being made to develop appropriate technologies that reduce climate change.

          1.4.3.3.4.4. Con:
          The fact that there is no technically feasible replacement globally for fossil fuel is largely due to the scale of energy use; humans could decrease their rampant consumption [736].

"In the case of climate science, there is a great deal of data because of the millions of daily observations made mostly for the purposes of weather forecasting. Climate scientists assemble all of the observations, including those made from satellites. They often make adjustments to accommodate known deficiencies and discontinuities, such as those arising from shifts in locations of observing stations or changes in instrumentation, and then analyze the data in various ways."
[4] https://www.newscientist.com/article/dn11641-climate-myths-chaotic-systems-are-not-predictable/
"Climate, however, is the bigger picture of a region’s weather: the average, over 30 years (according to the World Meteorological Association’s definition), of the weather pattern in a region. While weather changes fast on human timescales, climate changes fairly slowly. Getting reasonably accurate predictions is a matter of choosing the right timescale: days in the case of weather, decades in the case of climate."
[5] https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-021-22884-9.pdf
"None of the 222 scenarios in the IPCC SR1.5 and none of the shared socioeconomic pathways projects a declining GDP trajectory as is examined by the expanding degrowth literature."
[6] https://www.theguardian.com/commentisfree/2022/sep/12/global-heating-fighting-degree-target-2030
"Maybe we are too fixated with this precise temperature rise. The fact is, while not exactly picked out of a hat, the 1.5C figure is an arbitrary one. The exact level of temperature rise at which climate change becomes dangerous is simply not known."
[7] https://sciencing.com/advantages-large-sample-size-7210190.html
[8] https://history.aip.org/climate/simple.htm
"At the start of the 1960s, a few scientists began to think about transitions between different states of the oceans. Study of cores drilled from the seabed showed that water temperatures could shift more quickly than expected. A rudimentary model of ocean circulation constructed by Henry Stommel suggested that under some conditions only a small perturbation might shift the entire pattern of deep currents from one state to another. It was reminiscent of the shifts in the dishpan fluid models. All this was reinforced by the now familiar concept that fluctuations in ice sheets and snow cover might set off a rapid change in the Earth's surface conditions."
[9] https://agupubs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/epdf/10.1029/2018GL081046
"The feedback relationship between cloud cover and temperature is extremely important over northern Fennoscandia as can be seen in Figure S6.In winter, it is positive, with low‐level cloud cover acting to retain heat atthe surface. However, during the growing season, the cloud cover‐temperature relationship is a strongly negative one. Higher percentages of cloud cover lead to reduced summer temperatures and lower percen-tages increased temperatures. Cloud cover, therefore, plays an extremely important role in moderating surface temperature over Northern Fennoscandia, especially during summer months."
[10] https://www.acs.org/content/acs/en/climatescience/climatesciencenarratives/its-water-vapor-not-the-co2.html
"But cloud cover does mean more condensed water in the atmosphere, making for a stronger greenhouse effect than non-condensed water vapor alone – it is warmer on a cloudy winter day than on a clear one. Thus the possible positive and negative feedbacks associated with increased water vapor and cloud formation can cancel one another out and complicate matters. The actual balance between them is an active area of climate science research."

"In modeling terms, the difference between weather and climate is what we call an initial condition problem versus a boundary condition problem. Initial conditions are the starting point, the initial state of variables like wind, temperatures, pressure, and moisture. Boundary conditions, in contrast, are values prescribed by the modeler. Examples include the intensity of solar radiation and composition of the atmosphere. Weather depends on initial conditions, while climate on decadal and longer time scales depends primarily on boundary conditions."
[16] https://aeon.co/essays/all-scientific-models-are-wrong-but-some-at-least-are-useful
"It would be a shame if our odd relationship with economic models — can’t be doing with them, can’t live without them — produced a wider feeling that scientific models only reproduce the prejudices and predilections of the modeller. That is no more sensible than a readiness to embrace model projections simply because they result from computers whirring through lots of solutions to coupled equations, and are expressed in objective-looking numbers. Better to consider each kind of model on its merits. And that means developing a finer feel for the uncertainties in play, and how a particular research community handles them."

"Desertification is generally viewed as an advanced stage of land degradation. The UN has defined desertification as a “diminution or destruction of the biological potential of the land which can lead ultimately to desert-like conditions.” Desertification can occur when extended periods of drought in arid, semi-arid, or dry sub-humid areas — known as drylands — sap the land’s productivity until it becomes “dead” soil. In addition, human activities often contribute to the process. While people have managed fragile drylands successfully for millennia in many parts of the world, pressure upon the land is much greater today as roughly 2 billion people inhabit drylands around the world."
[19] https://www.climate.gov/news-features/understanding-climate/climate-change-global-temperature
"Given the tremendous size and heat capacity of the global oceans, it takes a massive amount of heat energy to raise Earth’s average yearly surface temperature even a small amount. The roughly 2-degree Fahrenheit (1 degrees Celsius) increase in global average surface temperature that has occurred since the pre-industrial era (1880-1900) might seem small, but it means a significant increase in accumulated heat.

That extra heat is driving regional and seasonal temperature extremes, reducing snow cover and sea ice, intensifying heavy rainfall, and changing habitat ranges for plants and animals—expanding some and shrinking others. As the map below shows, most land areas have warmed faster than most ocean areas, and the Arctic is warming faster than most other regions."

"Of all the papers, 75% fell into the first three categories, either explicitly or implicitly accepting the consensus view; 25% dealt with methods or paleoclimate, taking no position on current anthropogenic climate change. Remarkably, none of the papers disagreed with the consensus position.
Admittedly, authors evaluating impacts, developing methods, or studying paleoclimatic change might believe that current climate change is natural. However, none of these papers argued that point.
This analysis shows that scientists publishing in the peer-reviewed literature agree with IPCC, the National Academy of Sciences, and the public statements of their professional societies. Politicians, economists, journalists, and others may have the impression of confusion, disagreement, or discord among climate scientists, but that impression is incorrect."

[105] https://scholarlykitchen.sspnet.org/2017/04/11/what-constitutes-peer-review-research-data/
"As the availability of research data is increasing, it is important to ask how much of this data is peer reviewed. In a 2014 Study of 4,000 researchers by David Nicholas et al, “[i]t was generally agreed that data should be peer reviewed.”...
At the turn of the decade, a movement began to advance publication of research datasets as first class research objects. This has led to a variety of successful initiatives, such as the Joint Data Citation Principles and the FAIR Principles on data management, that aim to increase awareness and improve practice regarding the publication of datasets."
[106] https://undsci.berkeley.edu/article/howscienceworks_16
"Peer review does the same thing for science that the "inspected by #7" sticker does for your t-shirt: provides assurance that someone who knows what they're doing has double-checked it. In science, peer review typically works something like this:

 A group of scientists completes a study and writes it up in the form of an article. They submit it to a journal for publication.

 The journal's editors send the article to several other scientists who work in the same field (i.e., the "peers" of peer review).

 Those reviewers provide feedback on the article and tell the editor whether or not they think the study is of high enough quality to be published.

 The authors may then revise their article and resubmit it for consideration.

 Only articles that meet good scientific standards (e.g., acknowledge and build upon other work in the field, rely on logical reasoning and well-designed studies, back up claims with evidence, etc.) are accepted for publication."
[107] https://www.wiley.com/network/researchers/submission-and-navigating-peer-review/9-common-reasons-for-manuscript-rejection
"Major flaws might include a lack of clear control groups or other comparison metrics, non-conformity with recognized procedures or methodology (which makes it difficult to repeat or replicate the work), or the lack of a statistically valid analysis. Watch out for any minor flaws such as the incorrect, inappropriate, or unclear labeling of tables and figures."

[122] https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/pdf/10.1177/1747016119898400
"The scientific community has been largely successful in keeping criminal law away from the cases of research misconduct. Alleged cases of research misconduct are usually looked into by committees of scientists usually from the same institution or university of the suspected offender in a process that often lacks transparency. Few countries have or plan to introduce independent bodies to address research misconduct; so for the coming years, most universities and research institutions will continue handling alleged research misconduct cases with their own procedures. A global operationalization of research misconduct with clear boundaries and clear criteria would be helpful. There is room for improvement in reaching global clarity on what research misconduct is, how allegations should be handled, and which sanctions are appropriate."

"Methane has about 80 times the atmosphere-warming potential of carbon dioxide, though it tends to have a much shorter lifetime in the atmosphere — 10 to 20 years or so, compared with hundreds of years. The greenhouse gas can seep into the atmosphere from both natural and human-made sources (SN: 2/19/20).

In oil and gas production, massive methane bursts might be the result of accidents or leaky pipelines or other facilities, Lauvaux says. But these leaks are often the result of routine maintenance practices, the team found. Rather than shut down for days to clear gas from pipelines, for example, managers might open valves on both ends of the line, releasing and burning off the gas quickly. That sort of practice stood out starkly in satellite images as “two giant plumes” along a pipeline track, Lauvaux says."

""Correlation is not causation" means that just because two things correlate does not necessarily mean that one causes the other. As a seasonal example, just because people in the UK tend to spend more in the shops when it's cold and less when it's hot doesn't mean cold weather causes frenzied high-street spending. A more plausible explanation would be that cold weather tends to coincide with Christmas and the new year sales."

[137] https://www.scientificamerican.com/article/the-chaos-of-predicting-climate-change-video/
"Chaos theory is a field of mathematics that studies dynamic systems that can change drastically based on the smallest changes in their initial conditions. Because the future behavior of these systems is completely determined by their initial conditions, they are impossible to accurately predict. Weather and climate is a natural example of a deterministic, dynamic system. “If the flap of a butterfly’s wings can change the course of weather and climate,” Palmer wrote for the talk’s trailer, “what hope do we have trying to predict anything about climate 100 years hence?” Inexact supercomputing, which provides different results for the same input conditions, could be a way of modeling a chaotic system such as climate."

"The trace element selenium is essential for human health and is
required in a narrow dietary concentration range. Insufficient
selenium intake has been estimated to affect up to 1 billion people worldwide. Dietary selenium availability is controlled by soil–plant interactions, but the mechanisms governing its broad-scale soil distributions are largely unknown. Using data-mining techniques, we modeled recent (1980–1999) distributions and identified climate–soil interactions as main controlling factors. Furthermore, using moderate climate change projections, we predicted future (2080–2099) soil selenium losses from 58% of modeled areas (mean loss = 8.4%). Predicted losses from crop-lands were even higher, with 66% of croplands predicted to lose 8.7% selenium. These losses could increase the worldwide prevalence of selenium deficiency."

Indirectly, health may be damaged by ecological disruptions brought on by climate change (crop failures, shifting patterns of disease vectors), or social responses to climate change (such as displacement of populations following prolonged drought). Variability in temperatures is a risk factor in its own right, over and above the influence of average temperatures on heat-related deaths. Biological and social adaptation is more difficult in a highly variable climate than one that is more stable."

"We reviewed papers published between 1980 and 2014, and found 783 cases of excess human mortality associated with heat from 164 cities in 36 countries. Based on the climatic conditions of those lethal heat events, we identified a global threshold beyond which daily mean surface air temperature and relative humidity become deadly. Around 30% of the world’s population is currently exposed to climatic conditions exceeding this deadly threshold for at least 20 days a year. By 2100, this percentage is projected to increase to ∼48% under a scenario with drastic reductions of greenhouse gas emissions and ∼74% under a scenario of growing emissions."
[178] https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1228026
"“Current global temperatures of the past decade have not yet exceeded peak interglacial values but are warmer than during ~75% of the Holocene temperature history. Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change model projections for 2100 exceed the full distribution of Holocene temperature under all plausible greenhouse gas emission scenarios.”"
[179] https://www.theguardian.com/environment/2015/jun/23/weak-sun-could-offset-some-global-warming-europe-us-study
"Global warming in northern Europe and the eastern US could be partially offset in future winters because of the sun entering a weaker cycle similar to the one which enabled frost fairs to take place on the river Thames in the 17th and 18th century, according to new research.

However, the study said any potential weakening in solar activity would have only a small effect on temperature rises at a worldwide level, delaying the warming caused by emissions from cars, factories and power plants by around two years.

The sun has been in a period of high activity for the past few decades. But scientists believe there is now as much as a 20% chance of a weaker period of activity, known as a grand solar minimum, occurring in the next 40 years."

[181] https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/biodiversity-and-health
"People depend on biodiversity in their daily lives, in ways that are not always apparent or appreciated. Human health ultimately depends upon ecosystem products and services (such as availability of fresh water, food and fuel sources) which are requisite for good human health and productive livelihoods. Biodiversity loss can have significant direct human health impacts if ecosystem services are no longer adequate to meet social needs. Indirectly, changes in ecosystem services affect livelihoods, income, local migration and, on occasion, may even cause or exacerbate political conflict.

Additionally, biological diversity of microorganisms, flora and fauna provides extensive benefits for biological, health, and pharmacological sciences. Significant medical and pharmacological discoveries are made through greater understanding of the earth's biodiversity. Loss in biodiversity may limit discovery of potential treatments for many diseases and health problems."

"The Ice Age became truly bipolar around the (recently amended) Pliocene/Quaternary boundary, a little under three million years ago. The great Laurentide , Greenland and Scandinavian icecaps began to grow rapidly then, perhaps triggered by a ‘snowgun’ effect (where warm moisture-laden air blows across cold continental surfaces, triggering heavy snowfalls).

Prior to the Oligocene, and into the Mesozoic, the world had little or no polar ice (there is still debate as to the exact measure of ‘little or no’). Probably, there were small amounts of ice at least part of the time, for even in the late Cretaceous (generally regarded as a ‘greenhouse’ time) there were oscillations in sea level of a few tens of metres that seem best ascribed to the melting and re-forming of small polar icecaps."

[188] https://www.sciencedaily.com/releases/2013/08/130801142317.htm
"However, for other species, the effect of sea-ice loss could be just the opposite: "We know that, for some species, sea ice acts as a barrier to intermixing," Post explained. "So for these species, ice loss and a lengthening of the ice-free season likely will increase population mixing, reducing genetic differentiation." Post explained that, for example, polar and grizzly bears already have been observed to have hybridized because polar bears now are spending more time on land, where they have contact with grizzlies.

While such mixing of populations is not necessarily cause for concern, Post explained, it could lead to drastic changes in disease dynamics. For example, a population that currently is a host to a certain pathogen could carry that pathogen to another, previously unexposed population."

We validate the model using existing coral skeletal datasets from six Porites species collected across five reef sites and use this framework to project the impact of 21st century OA on Porites skeletal density across the global tropics.

"According to the international research team that examined how the increase in atmospheric CO2 over the past 170 years has likely changed the biochemistry of the two types of native famine weed plants, the answer lies in one of the organism’s less appealing traits. Famine weed produces a particularly nasty, carbon-based toxin called parthenin that prevents other plants from growing around it. It can also cause severe skin irritation and asthma in humans. This toxin is thought to contribute to the prolific spread of the plant by killing competing plants and possibly deterring predation."
[258] https://www.carbonbrief.org/climate-change-will-hit-endemic-plants-and-animals-the-hardest-study-warns
"Introduced species, on the other hand, can expect a “neutral or positive” impact from climate change, the study finds. Introduced species are often able to adapt to new environments, but sometimes have a negative impact on the local ecosystem – for example outcompeting native species for food. In this case, they are known as “invasive”.

Plants are “some of the world’s most proficient invasive species”, the study says, because they can often “outcompete” native species under increased temperatures and CO2 levels. In this study, plants made up the majority of introduced species in biodiversity hotspots."
[259] http://www.mobot.org/education/documents/aptl_plantandanimalinteractions.pdf
"Mutualism occurs when organisms of both species benefit from their
association. The relationship between pollinators and plants is a great example of mutualism. In this case, plants get their pollen carried from flower to flower and the animal pollinator (bee, butterfly, beetle, hummingbird, etc.) gets food (usually nectar or pollen). Seed dispersal is also often accomplished through a mutualistic relationship between the plant and its animal disperser. Many animals eat the sweet fruit that surrounds the seeds. The seeds are then deposited in a new location some distance away from the parent plant, often in fecal material which adds a little fertilizer as well!"

[261] https://core.ac.uk/download/pdf/219474611.pdf
"“ Climate change is a major concern for agriculture globally. Dynamic climatic parameters including increased temperature and carbon dioxide have greatly affected crop production. As a consequence of climatic uncertainties, new insect pests have emerged, the crop cultivation practices have changed, and drought and floods have created havoc around the globe. Besides, plant and insecticidal resistance against insects and diseases has got compromised, the diversity and abundance of arthropods has changed, geographical ranges of insect pests have extended far beyond their existing limits and new biotypes have evolved. All these have led to the reduced efficacy of crop protection technologies, huge crop losses, thereby, food insecurity.”"

Plants that can adjust their flowering times may also be better able to deal with higher temperatures. Jen Lau, a biologist at Indiana University Bloomington, suggests that this may give non-native plants the advantage when it comes to responding to climate change."
[263] https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/pdf/10.1080/1343943X.2015.1128114?needAccess=true
"In Japan, frequent extremely high temperatures during ripening have led to the deterioration of grain quality, especially after about the year 2000. Also when rice is exposed to high temperatures over a certain threshold during ripening, there is a decrease in the palatability of cooked rice. Furthermore, when air temperatures at flowering time exceed around 35°C under chamber experiment, heat-induced spikelet sterility occurs. Although actual damage in the open field had rarely been reported, the record heat reaching 40 °C in 2007 at the Kanto and Tokai regions in Japan induced substantial sterility, also around 35°C in summer of 2006 with high humid and low wind conditions induced spikelet sterility in hybrid rice grown at Jianghan Basin in China. Given that global warming is expected to continue, the powerful methods for preventing heat induced poor grain quality and yield loss are therefore required."

Given that developing countries in tropical regions will likely experience the biggest rise in hot days due to climate change, this adds yet another dimension to the climate change burden carried by low-income countries. The 10 countries most vulnerable to climate change identified by the University of Notre Dame’s index are all dealing with armed conflict. During the growing process, four elements are controlled – lighting, irrigation, fertigation and climate."

C3 photosynthetic plants possess a specific leaf structure, and are not adapted to non-optimal conditions."

Because VFs are often well-insulated and designed to operate day and night throughout the year, cooling is usually required 24/7 and year-round to remove the heat generated inside the space."

[296] https://globalecoguy.org/no-vertical-farms-wont-feed-the-world-5313e3e961c0
"Finally, indoor farms use a lot of energy and materials to operate. The container farms from Freight Farms, for example, use about 80 kilowatt-hours of electricity a day to power the lights and pumps. That’s nearly 2–3 times as much electricity as a typical (and still very inefficient) American home, or about 8 times the electricity used by an average San Francisco apartment. And on the average American electrical grid, this translates to emitting 44,000 pounds of CO2 per container per year, from electricity alone, not counting any additional heating costs. This is vastly more than the emissions it would take to ship the food from someplace else."

Additionally, supply and demand for fresh produce is not always consistent and pricing can change frequently, making it difficult to accurately predict returns on investment, which can be very problematic for a vertical farm that cost several million dollars to build. Furthermore, many of the processes required to grow crops cannot yet be addressed through off-the-shelf automation solutions, creating difficult engineering challenges that can make scale-up very complicated."

[302] http://www.a-ccpa.com/content/taxguide/text/c60s15d530.php
"Almost every business must invest in some major equipment, vehicles, machinery, fixtures or furniture in order to operate. Some businesses will require assets such as land, a building, patents, or franchise rights. Major assets that will be used in your business for more than a year are known as "capital assets" and are subject to special treatment under the tax laws. Most importantly, you generally can't deduct the entire cost of acquiring such an asset in the year you acquire it.

Why not? Because one of the goals of accounting is to accurately measure a business's gross income, expenses, and net income (earnings) during a given period of time, usually a year. If a business were allowed to reduce one year's gross income by an expense deduction for the total cost of an item that will be used for several years, the result would be an understatement of earnings in the year the asset was purchased, and an overstatement of earnings during the following years."

They are especially beloved for feasting on mosquitoes, midges and gnats, a service that can make it much easier to spend quality time in your garden or other outdoor spaces during the summer...

 As generalist predators, these amphibians may eat some beneficial insects like ladybugs, lacewings, or dragonflies. Yet herbivores often make easier prey, and since a single frog or toad may eat up to 100 insects per night, any crowds of leaf-eating pests in your garden would make a tempting feast. Frogs and toads devour all kinds of beetles, flies, moths, caterpillars, and other insect larvae, as well as slugs and snails, providing a powerful check on garden thieves. Salamanders have similar palates, eating like herbivores and also favoring mosquitoes and ticks."

"Roundup will kill almost any green plant that is actively growing . . . Take care to spray Roundup only on the weeds you want to kill--don’t allow the spray to contact plants you like or they may die too . . . Roundup should not be applied to bodies of water such as ponds, lakes or streams as Roundup can be harmful to certain aquatic organisms . . . After an area has been sprayed with Roundup, people and pets (such as cats and dogs) should stay out of the area until it is thoroughly dry . . . We recommend that grazing animals such as horses, cattle, sheep, goats, rabbits, tortoises and fowl remain out of the treated area for two weeks . . . If Roundup is used to control undesirable plants around fruit or nut trees, or grapevines, allow twenty-one days before eating the fruits or nuts."
[319] https://www.forestresearch.gov.uk/tools-and-resources/fthr/pest-and-disease-resources/dothistroma-needle-blight-dothistroma-septosporum/dothistroma-aerial-spraying-trials/
"Current methods of control involve clear-felling to reduce inoculum loads, changing thinning regimes within infected stands to open up the crop and decrease humidity, taking statutory action in tree nurseries to remove infected stock, and re-planting in the forest with less susceptible species where possible. In New Zealand however, aerial application of copper compounds is routinely carried out to manage the disease.

Copper is effective against Dothistroma septosporum as:

 copper deposits are slowly distributed over the needle surface and spores coming into contact with the copper ions are killed and fail to germinate
 copper stops fruiting bodies producing and releasing spores."

 Control and reduce the number of mosquitoes that can spread viruses. This can reduce your chances of getting sick.
 Control and reduce the number of nuisance mosquitoes that bother people but do not spread viruses."

[327] https://hbr.org/2016/04/when-economic-growth-doesnt-make-countries-happier
"There are multiple reasons to expect such increases in income inequality to cancel out and even reverse some of the positive effects of economic growth on well-being. First, inequality has been linked to lower levels of trust in others and a reduced sense of fairness; trust and fairness are both predictive of happiness. Second, income inequality is linked with fewer economic opportunities, less social mobility, poorer overall health, and higher levels of crime. All of these are causes for stress and anxiety, detracting from a society’s overall sense of safety, security, and well-being. Thus instances of the Easterlin Paradox may be more likely when economic growth is accompanied by growing income inequality."

In the UK, annual tonnage of particulate emissions dropped by more than 75 percent between 1970 and 2016, and of the main polluting chemicals by about 85 percent. Similar gains are common across the highest-income countries."

"Climate change creates new uncertainties about the spread of VBDs such as the Zika virus, dengue fever, malaria, and Lyme disease by altering conditions that affect the development and dynamics of the disease vectors and the pathogens they carry.

Rising global temperatures can lengthen the season and increase the geographic range of disease-carrying insects. As temperatures warm, mosquitoes and other warm-weather vectors can move into higher altitudes and new regions farther from the equator. For instance, in some regions in the United States, warming is lengthening the the season for Zika-carrying mosquitoes."
[341] https://www.independent.co.uk/news/science/climate-change-is-increasing-the-risk-of-malaria-for-people-living-in-mountainous-regions-in-the-9174448.html
"Scientists found that when temperatures increased, malaria affected people at higher altitudes than when temperatures were cooler. It is the first study on the ground to show how temperature changes can affect the risk of malaria in populations living 1,200 metres above sea level.

Although researchers have long debated whether malaria will move as a result of climate change, this is one of the first studies showing that the disease does migrate higher when temperatures rise, which threatens the many millions of people who live in historically malaria-free regions of the high-altitude tropics."
[342] https://www.breakdengue.org/impact-of-climate-change-on-dengue/

The rise in dengue cases is the result of a combination of two factors: global warming increases both the length of the dengue transmission season (as we have been seeing in Asia this year) and the number of geographical areas where the climate is suitable for dengue."

International Business Times explains that increasing temperatures means the number of days above 95 degrees Fahrenheit will increase from the past 30-year average of 39 days to 60 days by mid-century. By 2100, that number is expected to grow to 114 days. The increase in harsh conditions might impact production, as consistent exposure could affect worker safety.

Impact on Infrastructure

Scorching conditions aren’t the only changes rigs will have to ensure. Rising sea levels mean that the Gulf Coast will see its shoreline disappear by as much as two feet by mid-century. The rest of the Southeast will likely see higher sea levels, and will also see intensifying hurricanes. The Southeast and Gulf is home to many of the nation’s pipelines; intensifying flooding and winds could result in infrastructure damage and severely disrupt oil distribution."

countries are likely to suffer a greater adverse effect than rich countries from climate change because (a) they are more often exposed to very high temperatures; (b) their economies heavily rely on agriculture, natural resource extraction, and other sectors exposed to extreme weather variability; and (c) air conditioning, insurance, and other risk-management approaches are less available in poor countries than in rich countries."

[390] https://ethicsunwrapped.utexas.edu/glossary/veil-of-ignorance
"Philosopher John Rawls suggests that we should imagine we sit behind a veil of ignorance that keeps us from knowing who we are and identifying with our personal circumstances. By being ignorant of our circumstances, we can more objectively consider how societies should operate...

According to the liberty principle, the social contract should try to ensure that everyone enjoys the maximum liberty possible without intruding upon the freedom of others.

According to the difference principle, the social contract should guarantee that everyone an equal opportunity to prosper. In other words, if there are any social or economic differences in the social contract, they should help those who are the worst off. And, any advantages in the contract should be available to everyone."

A net 5 billion metric tons of human-produced carbon remain in the atmosphere each year, raising the global average carbon dioxide concentrations by about 2.3 parts per million per year. Since 1750, humans have increased the abundance of carbon dioxide in the atmosphere by nearly 50 percent. Learn more."

Many of the world’s poorest populations rely on farming or hunting and gathering to eat and earn a living — for example, Malawi is 80% agrarian. They often have only just enough food and assets to last through the next season, and not enough reserves to fall back on in the event of a poor harvest. So when climate change or natural disasters (including the widespread droughts caused by El Niño) leave millions of people without food, it pushes them further into poverty, and can make recovery even more difficult."

[405] http://www.nickbostrom.com/existential/risks.pdf
"The first manmade existential risk was the inaugural detonation of an atomic bomb. At the time, there was some concern that the explosion might start a runaway chain-reaction by “igniting” the atmosphere. Although we now know that such an outcome was physically impossible, it qualifies as an existential risk that was present at the time. For there to be a risk, given the knowledge and understanding available, it suffices that there is some subjective probability of an adverse outcome, even if it later turns out that objectively there was no chance of something bad happening. If we don’t know whether something is objectively risky or not, then it is risky in the subjective sense. The subjective sense is of course what we must base our decisions on. At any given time we must use our best current subjective estimate of what the objective risk factors are."

[412] https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-017-08467-z.pdf
"Ice-albedo feedback is a key aspect of global climate change. In the polar region, a decrease of snow and ice area results in a decrease of surface albedo, and the intensified solar heating further decreases the snow and ice area. In the Arctic Ocean, recent observations have revealed major reductions in summer ice extent, thinning of sea ice, and a shift from perennial to seasonal sea ice particularly after the 2000s. It is well established that climate change signals are amplified in the Arctic and that such “polar amplification” is associated with ice albedo feedbacks."

is dominated by the sensitivity of clouds to surface temperature and tropospheric stability. Considering changes in just these two factors, we are able to constrain global cloud feedback to 0.43 ± 0.35 W·m−2·K−1 (90% confidence), implying a robustly amplifying effect of clouds on global warming and only a 0.5% chance of ECS below 2 K."

This increase of total absorbed solar radiation contributes to continued and accelerated warming."

[418] https://www.axios.com/2020/08/31/climate-change-feedback-loops
"Water evaporation

This one’s more technical but far more consequential for Earth’s temperature than the AC example.

How it works: The atmosphere heats up as we emit heat-trapping greenhouse gases.

 This warmer air leads to more water evaporation from water and land.
 This evaporation results in water vapor, which itself is a greenhouse gas and traps heat.
 The increased amount of water vapor in the atmosphere retains ever more heat, which leads to more water evaporation, which results in more water vapor, which....

Between the lines: This type of feedback loop more than doubles the amount of global warming, says Hausfather."

Additional simulations show how optimal mitigation pathways evolve without imposing a warming limit, finding that median abatement levels and costs are not strongly dependent on start date. However, whereas 18(5) percent of optimal solutions starting in 1980 meet the 2(or 1.5) degree target, 5(or 0)% of 2020 simulations meet the goals.
